<meta>This page transitions from the general title page to the specific technical instructions of the astronomical commentary. It appears to describe the construction and calibration of a circular instrument (yantra) used for observing solar phenomena, likely an eclipse. The text discusses the placement of a gnomon (kīla) and the calculation of the solar disk's center.</meta>

Knowledge removes [uncertainty] for the sake of ease of use. By touching the foremost part of the practice, it is difficult to prevent [error] <note>This likely refers to the precision required in manual observation</note>... 

For that reason, the two indicators situated in the north, in the group related to the <term>circumference</term> <note>original: *paridhi*</note>... the hands are held there... the lines situated above at the desired location in the <term>drawn circle</term> <note>original: *kārṣamaṇḍalake*; likely a circle marked with ink or soot</note>. The <term>gnomon</term> <note>original: *kīla*; a central pin or rod used to cast a shadow or act as a pivot</note> in the circle should be equal to the <term>radius</term> <note>original: *vyāsārddha*</note>. 

Here is the layout: the gnomon is at the center of this circle. From the region between the circular lines to the white space, on both sides, East and West... In this grasping of the <term>eclipse</term> <note>original: *dharaṇa*; refers to the "seizing" of the sun or moon</note>, the appearance of the Sun in the uneven region... in this place of the eclipse, whatever is followed, there is <term>space</term> <note>original: *ākāśa*</note> on both sides. 

The middle part becomes black/inked... refined by the principle of the <term>central axis</term> <note>original: *merudaṇḍa*; literally "the staff of Meru," referring to the vertical axis of the instrument or the world</note>, the state of pervasiveness exists there. At the two ends of the line, joined with <term>calculation</term> <note>original: *kalpana*</note>, below the first position at a distance of six parts, below the <term>cross-hair</term> <note>original: *svastika*</note>... 

The calculation of the middle... elevated... he explains the method of knowledge. In this wheel, which is five-fold, the center of the <term>solar disk</term> <note>original: *sūryavimbakendra*</note> should be situated as it is in this wheel/leaf...

<meta>This page transitions from the physical setup of the astronomical instrument described on the previous page to the specific mathematical operations required to interpret the shadows it casts. It focuses on calculating the Sun's position—specifically its zenith distance—by measuring the length of the shadow from the central pin or gnomon.</meta>

<margin>Ti-Ti-Vi</margin> <note>These are likely abbreviations for technical time units or specific observation points, possibly *tithi* (lunar day) and *vighaṭī* (a unit of time equivalent to 24 seconds).</note> In the innermost part [of the instrument], the shadow of the <term>gnomon</term> <note>original: *kīla*; a central pin or rod used to cast a shadow</note> should be located in this section. Because of its multiple purposes, the position of the shadow there—in that location on the ground nearby—represents the intervening parts between the observers. This [line of] sight is the <term>zenith distance</term> <note>original: *natāṃśa*; the angular distance of a celestial body from the point directly overhead</note>, which is one [unit]. The center of the <term>solar disk</term> <note>original: *sūrya-vimba-kendra*</note> is positioned there.

In those three regional divisions, the visible portion is [measured] in <term>yojanas</term> <note>original: *yojana*; a traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 8 to 9 miles, used here to map celestial distances onto the instrument</note>. Furthermore, the distance between the various positions of the shadow is indeed called the zenith distance.

With the knowledge of the <term>right-angle line</term> <note>original: *sama-sūtra*; a fundamental alignment line used in Indian astronomy to ensure the instrument is level</note>, one should summarize [the data]. Having perfected the shadow, it is situated in its place. The shadow, measured in *yojanas*, is established in those positions. || 9 ||

The zenith distance multiplied by the [position of] the Sun's tip is explained in the four verses regarding the primary importance. From the position of the zenith distance, that same value is adjusted in the direction of the region. The Sun's center [is observed] through the <term>instrument</term> <note>original: *yantra*</note>. From the gnomon-pin at the top to the single line, and the tip of the shadow of the gnomon... || 9 ||

The measure of the face/opening, and so on... <margin>Night etc.</margin> <note>The marginal note indicates that these rules may also apply to nighttime observations, perhaps involving the moon or stars.</note> The <term>equinoctial shadow</term> <note>original: *palaprabhā*; the length of the shadow cast by a vertical gnomon at noon on the day of the equinox at a specific latitude</note> is multiplied by the gnomon. The result is separated by the difference. Following the path of the day, when there is equality, [the calculation] is completed.

8. In the same way, the zenith distance is the multiplier for the shadow. The result is produced by that measure. Here, the desired [value] is multiplied and destroyed <note>original: *nihantṛ*; in a mathematical context, this means to multiply or calculate out a value</note>...

From the movement [of the celestial bodies], just as it is determined in the middle position at the **tip of the shadow** <note>original: *chāyāgre*</note>, in that intervening space where one unit is found; by that, the [angular] degrees are calculated.

Here, the difference <unclear>sa na pe</unclear> or what is achieved... <note>The text here appears to discuss the correction of observed data</note> when the movement is processed, it is situated as it falls upon the **instrument** <note>original: *yantra*</note>.

That part which is... the part of the upper tip... such is the peg <note>original: *kīla*; likely referring to the pin of the instrument</note> in which... the form of the divisions of the sky [is shown].

From the division of those [parts]... the distance between the peg and the shadow... in the fifth middle... in the **shadow-tip**; the diameter of the instrument is so many digits <note>original: *aṅgula*; a standard unit of measurement roughly equal to a finger's width</note> in hands and spans <note>original: *hasta* and *vitasti*; traditional units of length</note>.

By the measurement of the instrument... the calculation is derived from the instrument's scale... with the peg [gnomon].

With the **hypotenuse** <note>original: *karṇa*; in this context, the diagonal line from the top of the gnomon to the tip of the shadow</note> in the form of a fraction... that **gnomon** <note>original: *śaṅku*</note> at the base is refined... for the purpose of the gnomon... at the base of the gnomon it falls.

Regarding the **equinoctial shadow** <note>original: *palaprabhā*; the midday shadow of a vertical gnomon on the day of the equinox, essential for calculating local latitude</note>—with a **twelve-digit gnomon** <note>original: *dvādaśāṅgula śaṅku*; the standard astronomical reference rod in Indian science</note>... where the sun provides... three and a half [units]... the gnomon falls. At the base of the gnomon, touching the **shadow-tips** in the **North** <note>original: *uttara*</note> and **South** <note>original: *dakṣiṇa*</note> directions, in that sequence... between the North and South... by that resulting figure... the gnomon...

<margin>**Siṃ. Vi. 2** <note>original: *Siṃ. Vi.*; abbreviation for a title such as *Siddhānta Vijaya*</note>. He who knows this nature again and again, knows the **peg** <note>original: *kīlaka*; a technical term for the gnomon or its fixed point</note> and the performer. This type [of knowledge] belongs to the three sets of eight <note>likely referring to 24 units or divisions</note> known as the **Great Rudra of Knowledge** <note>original: *jñānamahārudra*; potentially the name of this specific computational method or text</note>.</margin>

Regarding the peg... by the **gnomon** <note>original: *śaṅku*; a vertical rod used to measure shadows</note>, the result is obtained. By that result of the *Great Rudra of Knowledge*, the fruit is not [reached]. Here, if the result is less than the surface of the peg, it is for the purpose of the remainder of the directions.

From the shadow... the north shadow... the **planets** <note>original: *graha*</note>... knowledge of the **south** <note>original: *dakṣiṇa*</note>. Otherwise, when the result is diminished... 

By joining the directions... from the south, the knowledge of the **north** <note>original: *uttara*</note> is [gained]. [8] By these nine **verses** <note>original: *śloka*</note>, the calculation of time by the *Great Rudra*... [5]... The **declination** <note>original: *krānti*; the sun’s distance north or south of the celestial equator</note> divided by the **hypotenuse** <note>original: *karṇa*; the diagonal line from the gnomon tip to the shadow tip</note> is subtracted from the difference. [9]

Having established the **radius** <note>original: *trijyā*; literally "three-jivas," the standard radius of 3438 minutes used in Indian trigonometry</note> for the purpose of the *Great Rudra*... the declination is placed. [10] Multiplied by the forms... by the radius...

Multiplied by the qualities... with the sun to be determined... the parts of the difference from the radius... the day... *Great Rudra*. [2 m 7, 11] Deprived of the calculation equal to the observation... declared from the sky... 

...the **equinoctial hypotenuse** <note>original: *palakarṇa*; the hypotenuse of the midday shadow at the equinox</note>. In the hypotenuse, **twelve is the base** <note>original: *dvādaśa koṭi*; standard Indian gnomons are exactly 12 digits high, forming the "base" of the calculation</note>... what is the base in the hypotenuse... the **sine of the declination** <note>original: *krānti jyā*</note>...

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical derivation of solar positions. Following the establishment of the twelve-digit gnomon on the previous page, this section focuses on the calculation of the sine of declination and the ascensional difference to determine the "Swan" (haṃsa), a technical geometric figure used to find the sun's direction.</meta>

Therefore, from the **sine of declination** <term>krāntijyā; the angular distance of the Sun north or south of the celestial equator</term>, with the **twelve-digit gnomon** <note>original: *dvādaśa-koṭī*; referring to the standard vertical pillar used for shadow measurements</note> acting as the side, the result is the **base** <term>bhuja; the horizontal side of a right-angled triangle</term>. From that sine of declination, the **earth-sine** <term>kujyā; the distance between the rising-setting line and the diurnal circle's diameter</term> is produced by the **radius** <term>trijyā; literally "three-jivas," the radius of the circle used in calculations</term>. He now explains the calculation of the **ascensional difference** <term>carajyā; the difference between the local and equatorial rising times</term> and the **Swan** <note>original: *haṃsa*; likely referring to a specific point or solar parameter in the *Siddhānta* system</note>... 

Twelve is the **base** <note>the gnomon height</note> and the hand is the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa; the diagonal measurement from the gnomon's tip to the shadow's edge</term>. From that, the **sine of the zenith distance** <term>nata-aṃśa-jyā; the angular distance from the point directly overhead</term> is the **gnomon** <note>original: *śaṅku*</note>... base... hypotenuse... radius... This establishes the method of the Swan mentioned previously. 

By this, the desired middle... beginning from the East and so on, the **South-East** <note>original: *āgneyī*</note> is established. Similarly, by the **radius** <note>original: *triguṇa*</note>, as stated in the **Chapter on the Three Problems** <term>Tri-praśna-adhikāra; a standard section in Indian astronomical texts dealing with time, direction, and place</term>, the ascensional difference... is diminished by the **versed sine** <term>utkramajyā; a trigonometric function (1 - cosine)</term> of the **hour angle** <term>natakāla; the time elapsed before or after noon</term>. 

From that cause, the remaining sine is positive. Subtracted from the **midday** <note>original: *dinārddha*; literally "half-day"</note>... twelve... 

He explains the calculation of the **sine of declination** <note>original: *krāntijyā*</note>. The sine of declination is multiplied by the **radius** and divided by the sine of the maximum declination. This should be subtracted... the second Sun... 24 degrees... the sine of the maximum declination <note>original: *caturviṃśati-jyā*; the sine of 24 degrees, the traditional Indian value for the obliquity of the ecliptic</note>.

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical derivation of solar positions. While the previous page established the geometric "Swan" (haṃsa) figure using the gnomon and shadows, this section moves into the calculation of the Sun’s true longitude (spashta-surya) by converting the sine of declination into an arc and applying seasonal corrections based on the zodiac signs.</meta>

<margin>Siddhānta [Siddhāñjana] 3</margin> Then, from the maximum **sine of declination** <term>parama-krāntijyā; the sine of the maximum angle (approx. 24°) between the ecliptic and the equator</term>, which is compared to the **radius** <term>trijyā; the standard radius used in Indian trigonometry, often 3438 minutes</term>, if the base is equal to the sine of declination, then what is the value of the declination? From that, the **arc** <term>dhanu; literally "a bow," referring to the arc-length derived from a sine value</term> is determined. 

If the Sun is in the **first quadrant** <note>0° to 90° from the vernal equinox</note>, the calculated arc itself is the Sun’s position. In the **second quadrant** <note>90° to 180°</note>, the arc should be subtracted from the half-circle <note>180°</note> to find the Sun’s longitude. In the **third quadrant** <note>180° to 270°</note>, one should add the arc to the half-circle <note>180°</note>. In the **fourth quadrant** <note>270° to 360°</note>, the arc should be subtracted from the full circle <note>360°</note>. 

From this, the corrected Sun is revealed. When this value is adjusted by the **precession of the equinoxes** <term>ayanāṃśa; the longitudinal difference between the tropical and sidereal zodiacs</term>, the **True Sun** <note>original: *spashta-surya*</note> **is established.** 

By reversing this process, starting from the **Aries** <term>Mesha</term> point, the position becomes clear. This calculation varies depending on the specific location <note>original: *pratideśa-jāni-bhinnā*; acknowledging that astronomical observations change based on the observer's latitude</note>. The precessional correction is applied to the signs starting from Aries. These are associated with the **six seasons** <note>original: *ṣaḍ-ṛtavaḥ*</note>. <margin>Rama [Scribe/Author]</margin>

The relationship between the signs of the **ecliptic** <term>krāntivṛtta; the Sun's apparent path</term> and the seasons of the solar year is as follows:

| Zodiac Signs | Season |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Aries** and **Taurus** <note>Mesha and Vrisha</note> | **Spring** <note>Vasanta; during the months of Chaitra and Vaishakha</note> |
| **Gemini** and <unclear>Cancer</unclear> <note>Mithuna and Karka</note> | **Summer** <note>Grīṣma</note> |
| **Leo** and **Virgo** <note>Simha and Kanya</note> | **Monsoon** <note>Varṣā</note> |
| **Libra** and **Scorpio** <note>Tula and Vrishchika</note> | **Autumn** <note>Śarad</note> |
| **Sagittarius** and **Capricorn** <note>Dhanu and Makara</note> | **Pre-Winter** <note>Hemanta</note> |
| **Aquarius** and **Pisces** <note>Kumbha and Mīna</note> | **Winter** <note>Śiśira</note> |

Therefore, the year is divided by the transit of the Sun through these signs. In the spring, when the Sun occupies Aries and Taurus, the months are known as Chaitra and Vaishakha...

<summary>This page describes the trigonometric method for calculating the Sun's true longitude across the four quadrants of the celestial circle and maps the twelve zodiac signs to the six traditional Indian seasons.</summary>
<keywords>True Sun (Spashta Surya), Sine of Declination (Kranti-jya), Precession (Ayanamsha), Zodiac Signs, Six Seasons (Ritu), Indian Astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the mathematical calculation of the Sun’s position (longitude) to a philosophical and geographical discussion regarding the "signs" of the seasons. It addresses a potential contradiction: if astronomical calculations are universal, why do the physical signs of seasons (like flowering trees) differ from place to place? It specifically cites the authority of Bhaskaracharya’s *Siddhānta Shiromaṇi* while grounding those descriptions in the geography of North-Central India.</meta>

...regarding the rains and other seasons. One might ask: how can there be a fixed understanding of these seasons given the lack of uniform signs? The **signs of the seasons** <term>ṛtu-chihna; the physical indicators like blooming flowers or weather patterns that define a season</term>, such as those for **Spring** <term>Vasanta</term>, are established based on the specific characteristics of each country. Because these signs are perceived differently from region to region, they are based on local observation, tradition, and the repeated association of various natural phenomena in the world.

Therefore, because there are no "universal" seasonal signs that apply to every country, one might think that regional observations are contradictory or confused. The essence of the argument here is to prevent such confusion when observing the different markers of time across various lands. <note>verse 97</note>

An objection is raised: "If that is the case, then why did **Bhāskara** <note>Bhāskarācārya, the 12th-century mathematician and astronomer</note> and others describe these signs in works such as the **Siddhānta Shiromaṇi** <term>The "Crown of Treatises"; a foundational text of Indian astronomy written in 1150 CE</term>?" 

In response, the following verses describe the fresh sprouts of the **Mango tree** <term>sahakara; the fragrant mango tree, often used as a symbol of spring</term>, the gathering of dark clouds, and the abundance of golden flowers:

**Signs of trees and nature** were described by Bhāskara and the learned scholars who preceded him specifically as they appear in the **Middle Country** <term>Madhyadeśa; the central region of the Indo-Gangetic plain, considered the cultural and scientific heart of ancient India</term>. They described the sequence of the seasons—starting with Spring—according to how they are observed there. The previous verses describing these signs should be understood in this context. For example, the appearance of new sprouts marks **Spring** <note>original: *vasantādi*</note>, while the approach of dark, water-laden clouds occurs when the seasons transition toward the monsoon.

<summary>This page discusses the geographical relativity of seasonal signs, explaining that while astronomical seasons are calculated mathematically, the botanical and climatic signs described by Bhaskaracharya refer specifically to Central India (Madhyadesha).</summary>
<keywords>Bhaskaracharya, Siddhanta Shiromani, seasonal signs (ritu-chihna), Madhyadesha, Spring (Vasanta), regional variation</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 3 regarding the geographical specificity of seasonal markers. It addresses the tension between the "universal" astronomical definitions of seasons found in the Siddhāntas and the local botanical or climatic reality, specifically contrasting the heartland of North India with the mountainous region of Kashmir.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>

<margin>
Siddhānta Śiromaṇi <note>original: "Si. Si."; an abbreviation for the 12th-century astronomical text by Bhāskarācārya</note>
</margin>

...possessing a purity like the nourishing beams of a silver moon. The rising of new crops serves as the **sign of Early Winter** <term>Hemanta-cihna; the indicators of the season between autumn and mid-winter</term>. Some signs are described as being internal to the body, while others are external.

Regarding the **Middle Country** <term>Madhya-desha; the central region of Northern India, traditionally the area between the Himalayas and the Vindhya mountains</term>, it is stated that the geographic limits are defined by the meridian line extending as far as the **Prayag line** <note>The longitude of Prayagraj, used as a reference for astronomical calculations</note>. Within this specific region, the previously mentioned signs are determined.

In poetry and specialized discourse, **Spring** <term>Vasanta</term> is the most prominent and principal among the seasons. The various seasons have been described in sequence by the scholar **Bhāskara** <note>Bhāskarācārya</note> and other learned teachers in their astronomical treatises. These descriptions are given as regional indicators. 

However, one might ask: do these signs apply to all lands? It is said by some that in the land of **Kashmir** <margin>Rāma</margin> <note>Possibly a reference to a commentator or a local authority</note>, these specific signs are not present. Because of the excessive cold and snow, things such as the blooming of mango trees <note>original: "rasāla-mra"; referring to the fragrant mango blossom typical of spring in the plains</note> do not occur there.

This is the definition of Spring: these signs are what will occur in the future as described by the wise. But for the land of Kashmir, a different indicator is used, as these signs do not manifest in the **Western regions**. The reason for this is given: in the land of Kashmir, there is **perpetual snow** <term>sadā-himā; the state of being constantly covered by frost or snow, which alters the local botanical calendar</term>.

<summary>This page explains that the traditional signs of the seasons (like mango blossoms in spring) are based on the geography of Central India (Madhya-desha) and do not apply to regions like Kashmir, where perpetual snow creates a different climatic reality.</summary>

<keywords>Kashmir, Spring, Early Winter, Bhāskara, Siddhānta, Middle Country, Prayag, seasonal signs, climate relativity</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous folio regarding the regional limitations of seasonal markers. It transitions from a geographical and botanical critique of standard poetic tropes (specifically why Central Indian descriptions of spring do not apply to Kashmir) into a technical manual on astronomical observation using sighting instruments.</meta>

...due to the constant cold. Because of the absence of mango trees <note>original: *rasāla*; the mango tree is the quintessential symbol of spring in Indian poetry, which the author argues is missing in the north</note>, the intense desire for the season and so forth mentioned in the texts is never experienced in the land of **Kashmir**. Indeed, because of the constant presence of snow and cold, and the lack of mangoes, such intense longings and descriptions have never been known by me in the land of **Kashmir**. [Verse] 17

Now, regarding the **five-fold observation** <term>pañca-vedha; a technical method of astronomical sighting or "piercing" the position of a planet</term>, he speaks in two verses regarding the **visual location** <term>dṛk-sthāna</term>:

### Verse 18
...The planetary disk <term>graha-bimba</term> is situated on the **pin** <term>kīlaka; the central bolt or sighting pin of an astronomical instrument</term>... like a flower... [one rim]... indeed, these five... [situated] in the northern latitude...

Observe now: the planetary disk is situated at the central part between the two **rims** <term>nemi; the circular framework or "felly" of a sighting instrument</term> of the **instrument** <term>yantra</term>. Like a wheel, the planet moves... its outer boundary... "That is the planet," so one should observe in the instrument. 

[Verse] 3 

The stars such as **Revatī**, **Puṣya**, and **Maghā** <note>These are specific Lunar Mansions or Nakṣatras used as reference points in the sky</note>... the **junction-stars** <term>yoga-tārā; the brightest or most significant star in a constellation used for astronomical calculations</term>... touching the rim... the planetary disk in the month of **Caitra** <note>The first month of the Hindu calendar, roughly March-April</note>...

...held in that portion. For the purpose of establishing such a position, the planetary disk remains visible, aligned in a **straight line** <term>sama-sūtra; literally "equal thread," referring to a perfectly horizontal or vertical alignment</term> with the sighting pin. One should then observe whether the portion of the rim north of the pin is directed toward the north or the south. At that time, the planetary disk is in a straight line with the rim; the planetary disk is joined with the pin, aligned and unified...

<margin>Detailed explanation of the Siddhānta-Bhāskara <note>This likely refers to a commentary or a specific section of a larger astronomical work, possibly related to the works of Bhāskara</note></margin>

The rim of the instrument is desired to represent the fixed path of the planetary motion... [within] the seven... with the movement of the fixed pole... in the **central region** <term>madhya-deśa; historically referring to the central part of the Indian subcontinent, used as a reference for longitudinal calculations</term>. By understanding the variation in the **planetary shadow** <term>graha-chāyā</term>, one determines the true movement.

In this sequence, the actual path of the planetary motion is established. **[Verse] 19**

Now he speaks of the **point of contact** <term>sparśa-sthāna; the specific coordinate where the line of sight meets the planet's position on the instrument</term>. At the place where the **sighting pin** <term>kīlaka</term> is located, <unclear>one should observe</unclear>... the alignment of the planet. For the observation made in the forenoon, directed toward the pin, the resulting arc of the point of contact on the **planetary disk** <term>graha-bimba</term> <note>original: *graha-vimbadirk*; the visible circular face of a planet or the sun</note> is calculated. 

At the time of the **five-fold observation** <term>pañca-kāla-vedha</term>, the position of the pin is aligned with the planet. One should calculate the **arrow-sine** <term>śara-jyā; the "sagitta" or the height of an arc segment, a fundamental trigonometric value in Indian astronomy</term> by multiplying the measure of the sighting pin by three and seven <note>This refers to a specific mathematical ratio or constant used in the calculation of chords and arcs</note>. By adding or subtracting this value, the exact portion of the planetary disk is determined. **[Verse] 20**

He then speaks of the **elevation of the planet** <term>grahochitā</term>. With the knowledge of the Sun's position... <margin>Scribe's name: Rāma</margin> the arc is calculated on the level ground. When the planet is placed upon the instrument, the contact and the elevation are famously known... between the two parts of the rim... the altitude of the planet is found through the **shadow of the sighting pin** <term>kīla-chāyā</term>.

From the possibility of [observation], both [values] are reached in this way; this is desired... **21** ...having gone through the three-fold path, the desired result is stated.

From the **true declination** <term>sphuṭa-apakrama; the angular distance of a celestial body north or south of the celestial equator, corrected for specific orbital variations</term> and other factors, the calculation is to be accomplished through the refined measurement. Thus, speak of the refined position <note>original: *saṃskṛtam*; refers to a mathematically "corrected" or "purified" value</note> of the planet.

**22** The days and the time passed therein, and how much of the night has passed, that is the temporal state. From that time, again, the same [process is followed]... from the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa; the diagonal line of sight or the calculated result of the shadow's triangle</term>, the **zenith distance** <term>nata-bhāga; the angular distance of the planet from the point directly overhead</term> is determined.

In this manner, by the method of the Sun, the planets are known. The **true position** <term>sphuṭa</term> is refined. When the planet is at the [sighting] hypotenuse, its own path is established. The **true declination** <note>original: *spaṣṭa-krānti*</note> should be calculated, as well as the **mean declination** <note>original: *madhya-sādhā*</note> from these values.

Here, following the direction of its own declination... the planetary motion is seen when the true declination is calculated. From the hypotenuse obtained through this observation, the **hour angle of the planet** <term>nata-kāla; the time elapsed since the planet crossed the local meridian</term> is found. 

By subtracting this from **midday** <term>dinārddha; literally "half-day"</term>, the [current] time is found. In the **eastern hemisphere** <term>pūrva-kapāla; the half of the sky where celestial bodies rise</term>, the hour angle represents the time before noon; in the **western result** <term>apara-phala</term>, the hour angle indicates the time past noon. 

Then, through the **sighting correction** <term>dṛk-saṃskṛti; a correction applied to account for the observer's specific visual perspective</term>, as performed previously, the true **ascendant** <term>lagna; the sign of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon</term> and the temporal calculations are established.

### Investigation into the Essence of the Siddhānta

<margin>Investigation into the Essence of the Siddhānta</margin>

From those eighty-six <note>original: *ṣaḍaśīti*; likely referring to the *Ṣaḍaśīti-mukha*, an astronomical period or a specific correction factor</note> [units], the **calculated ascendant** <term>sādhita-lagna; the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at a specific time</term> is determined. In the manner described for the planetary positions in the yearly almanac, the **desired time** <term>iṣṭa-kāla; the specific time elapsed since sunrise or the moment of observation</term> is to be calculated. Here, the desired time should be based on the **sidereal measure** <term>nakṣatra-māna; time calculated based on the rotation of the Earth relative to the fixed stars</term>. Because of the calculation involving the **civil/solar measure** <term>sāvana-māna; time based on the Earth's rotation relative to the Sun</term>, there is a difference in the results.

From that time, according to the characteristics of the calculation... in the refined eighty-six calculations of the **ascendant** <note>original: *lagna*</note>, the second form of the time is considered most appropriate. This is the calculated result of the solar day... **23** ...In this way, for that reason, the directions should be followed according to the path described in the yearly almanac. Following the aforementioned method, the position of the **night elapsed** <note>original: *rātri-gata*; the portion of the night that has already passed at the time of observation</term> should be determined. Thus, even if there is an absence of the **latitude/deflection** <term>śara; literally 'arrow', refers to the celestial latitude of a planet</term>, the position on the **ecliptic** <term>kānti-vṛtta; the Sun's apparent path across the sky</term> remains central. Even if the calculation of the night elapsed from the ascendant is possible, this is the intended method.

Regarding the fourth [section], he describes the form of the **Quadrant** <term>turya-yantra; an astronomical instrument used for measuring altitudes and time, consisting of a 90-degree arc</term>:

<margin>Rāma</margin>

Now, the form of this **tilted instrument** <term>trasta-yantra; a specific type of portable or pivoted sighting device</term> is a quadrant. Its head, upper circumference, and corners are marked with the directions of space. There are transverse lines. From those lines, at the corners of the circumference, is the **horizon line** <term>kṣitija-rekhā</term>. Below the horizon line, on the upper circumference and the sides, apertures <note>original: *chidra*; small holes for sighting</note> should be made for easy observation. Between the horizon and the zenith <note>original: *kha-madhya*</note>, in the section of the circumference, at nine parts <note>original: *nava-aṃśa*; nine degrees</term>, the **sunlight** <note>original: *arka-tejaḥ*</note> enters simultaneously through the apertures.

### The Application of the Quadrant Instrument

...and in this **instrument** <term>yantra; a mechanical device used for astronomical observations</term>, even when it is used for a period of **three years** <note>original: *saṃvatsaratraya*; this may refer to a cycle of corrections or the durability of the instrument's calibration</note>, a correction of sixty-five [units] is applied. Within that, the parts of the **circumference** <term>paridhi; the outer boundary or perimeter of the circular scale</term> represent the **altitude** <note>original: *uccatā*; the angular height of a celestial body above the horizon</note>. 

In that context, following this method, the calculation is performed for the **day elapsed** <note>original: *dinagata*; the time passed since sunrise</note> during the northern course of the sun. It is said that this was not explicitly stated before, but now it is declared as "well-known." Indeed, in the famous **Quadrant instrument** <term>tūryayantra; a specialized tool representing one-fourth of a circle, used to measure altitudes and determine time</term>, this was not mentioned in such a way, although it is obtained through the established rules of observation.

In the **accurate instrument** <note>original: *spaṣṭa-yantra*; likely referring to a refined or corrected version of the quadrant</note>, this is indicated for the purpose of making the **three hundred** <note>original: *triśata*; likely referring to 300 divisions or degrees on a specific scale</note> divisions known. However, at the end of this work, the remaining tasks are explained. The idea is that those skilled only in logic did not understand the minute divisions of the parts; having performed these calculations, the essence should be known, as it was previously neglected. **25**

This same point is clarified concisely: the various positions and divisions of all quadrants are clearly described in the **Jewel of Instruments** <term>Yantracintāmaṇi; a famous 16th-century Sanskrit treatise on astronomical instruments by the scholar Cakradhara</term>. Therefore, it should be understood that the "five centers" have been mentioned for this reason. **25**

By inserting the **pin** <term>kīlaka; a gnomon or central peg used to cast a shadow or act as a pivot</term> and considering the arrangement of the frame, the parts become clear. Because of their variations and refinements, the text of the **Jewel of Instruments** was composed based on the principles of **planetary mathematics** <term>graha-gaṇita; the branch of astronomy dealing with the mathematical tracking of planetary motions</term>. Its essence is clear. For the sake of ease of use, in the calculation of the Quadrant instrument, one should imagine the position of the **center** <note>original: *kendra*</note>... having placed the line within the world...

<header>9</header>

<margin>Commentary on the Siddhanta Samrat</margin> <note>original: *si. sā. vi.*, likely abbreviating *Siddhānta-Samrāṭ-Vivaraṇa*</note>

...the measured **time-units** <term>nāḍī; a unit of time equivalent to 24 minutes</term>, then from the **sine-axis** <term>jīvākṣa; the axis used for calculating trigonometric sines</term> and the sequence of intervals. In the center of the circle, the **half-angle** <note>original: *koṇārddhaḥ*</note> is formed at the sun's position. That line also represents the movement of the center's radius, whether in **retrograde** <term>vakrī; the apparent backward motion of a planet</term> or direct motion upon the **horizon** <term>kṣitija; the circle where the earth meets the sky</term>.

The lines originating from the center are designated as the **Earth** <term>bhūmi; the horizontal base line of the instrument</term>. Within the diagonals, the **Sky-Mountain** <note>original: *ākāśaparvata*; likely referring to the vertical zenith point or the 90-degree marker on the quadrant</note> is divided into **ninety equal parts** <note>original: *navatirvibhāgāḥ samāḥ*; these represent the 90 degrees of altitude from horizon to zenith</note>. 

From the **Sky** <note>the zenith</note> to the **Mountain of Duration** <note>original: *sthitigeya-parvata*</note>, there are **ten equal ghatikas** <note>approximately 4 hours of time</note>. Starting from the Earth-line and extending to the **rim** <term>nemi; the outer edge of the quadrant</term>, the **Line of Cancer** <term>karkajīrekhā; the line representing the Sun's position at the Summer Solstice</term> is shaped like the Sky-line. 

In this manner, there are **thirty-five sines** <term>jīvā; chords used in Indian trigonometry</term> placed at equal intervals. The space between these sines is measured as one **digit** <term>aṅgula; a traditional unit of length roughly equal to the width of a finger</term>. In this region, one **minute of arc** <term>kalā; 1/60th of a degree</term> corresponds to the sine. From the center, the divisions of the **latitude** <term>palāṃśa; the degrees of latitude of the observer's location</term> are to be understood in the sky-divisions. These are to be known through both the regular and inverse sequences. <margin>Calculation of Rama</margin> 

From the **horizon-shadow** <note>original: *kṣitija-chāyā*</note>, the eighteen interval lines are marked with the total number of digits, measuring **twenty-seven and a half digits** <note>original: *sārdhasaptaviṃśatyaṅgulā*</note>. From the top of that, where the shadow falls, a cord is extended from the center to the circumference. From the point where it intersects the rim, following its own path to the **Mountain of Position** <note>the fixed marker</note>, there are **six divisions** <note>original: *ṣaḍtōṃśāḥ*</note>. At the corner of the Sky-Mountain, there are likewise six divisions or parts in the sequence.

<meta>This page continues the technical geometric instructions for the Tūryayantra (Quadrant). It transitions from the general division of the instrument's arc to the specific calculation and marking of the sine lines (jyā) on the horizontal "Earth" line and vertical "Sky" line.</meta>

Regarding the **horizon** <term>kṣitija; the horizontal baseline of the instrument</term>, when observing the **total sine** <term>samastajyā; the chord or full sine</term> on the **Earth-surface** <note>original: *bhūmau*; referring to the base of the quadrant</note>, the **Earth-line** <term>bhūrekhā</term> extending to the edge of the horizon is considered one unit of measure for that region.

The **R-sine** <term>kramajyā; the standard trigonometric sine used in Indian astronomy</term> is similarly determined. From the term "sky-direction-degrees," the total sine is observed on the base. Starting from that point and extending to the **center** <term>kendra</term>, the Earth-line constitutes one unit of measurement for the R-sine. The **half-earth-line** <note>original: *arddhaca-bhūrekhā*</note>... its measure as a sine is self-evident. Otherwise, in its natural position, the **horizon-sines** <term>kṣitijyā</term> are indeed the R-sines. Each of these is marked in **digits** <term>aṅgula; a standard unit of measurement, roughly a finger-breadth</term>, and these must be known by the practitioner.

The **sine-lines** <term>jyārekhā</term> are not all established by simple measurement. In this way, the **Sky-line** <term>ākāśarekhā; the vertical axis representing the zenith</term>... provides the successful calculation of the R-sine. 

Because of the eighteen sines, twelve **digits** <note>the standard length of a gnomon (shadow-casting pole) in Indian astronomy is 12 digits</note> remain from the center... This is the **altitude-complement** <term>koṭi; the vertical side of the right-angled triangle</term>. From the eighteen sines, seven parts are taken; these constitute the **sine-arm** <term>bhuja; the horizontal side of the triangle</term>. The **equinoctial shadow** <term>palabhā; the shadow cast by a vertical gnomon at noon on the day of the equinox</term> of the object at the center extends to that point. 

The sine is the "arm" (*bhuja*). This Earth-line extending to the center is situated within the **circumference** <term>paridhi</term> of the astronomical circle. This is established through careful effort and observation...

From the middle of the center... the parts are made manifest... <note>The final lines of the transcription are fragmentary and appear to describe the physical engraving or manifestation of the degree marks on the instrument's surface.</note>

<margin>
Siddhānta-śiromaṇi-vivaraṇa
<note>This refers to a detailed commentary on Bhāskara II’s famous 12th-century astronomical treatise.</note>
8
</margin>

...measured by the local **equinoctial shadow** <term>palabhā; the shadow cast by a vertical pole at noon on the day of the equinox</term> in one's own region. In the center-plane, the **declination** <term>krānti; the sun's distance north or south of the celestial equator</term> is calculated by the [sine] of the **longitude** <term>bhuja</term>. Through the union of the two, the **sine** <term>jyā</term> is obtained. By the measure of the **radius-sine** <term>trijyā; the standard radius used in trigonometric calculations</term>, the **Maximum Sine of Declination** <term>paramakrāntijyā; the sine of the angle of the Earth's tilt, roughly 24 degrees in ancient Indian astronomy</term> is given as 12 | 11 | 53 <note>These figures represent degrees, minutes, and seconds in a sexagesimal system used for precise measurement</note>. 

Accepting this measurement, and because the **center-point** <term>kendra-sthāna</term> is the origin of the **unit-sine** <note>original: *rūpajīvā*; literally "one-sine" or the standard unit</note>, one should proceed. Starting from the center-place, using the measure of the **radius-sine** <term>nijyā</term>, the position of the maximum declination is established. From the two points established from the center—relating to the **Sky-line** <term>ākāśarekhā; the vertical axis</term> and the **Earth-line** <term>bhūrekhā; the horizontal axis</term>—one finds the position on the **circumference** <term>paridhi</term> by means of a straight line, like a knee-joint <note>original: *jānukāreṇa*; likely referring to an angular or bent-line geometric construction</note>.

From that point, up to the limit of the Sky-line, the desired **Declination-sine** <term>krāntijyā</term> is found. By establishing the **Sine of Longitude** <term>bhujajyā</term> as the "side" <note>the horizontal base of a right-angled triangle</note> and the **Radius-sine** <term>trijyā</term> as the "hypotenuse" <note>original: *karṇa*</note>, the Maximum Sine of Declination is derived. 

<margin>M:</margin> 

By the falling of the hypotenuse at the center-point, the Declination-sine is marked. From the limit of the Sky-line, which is formed by a straight line, the **Declination-sine** <term>krāntijyā</term> meets the circumference at a specific point. Because of this, the Maximum Sine of Declination is marked from the Sky-line using a straight line to the circumference...

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical derivation for the Tūryayantra (Quadrant) construction. It transitions from defining the maximum declination to the geometric "proof" or "derivation" (atropattiḥ) of how the gnomon’s shadow relates to the sine and cosine of the sun's position, specifically when the sun is in the southern celestial hemisphere.</meta>

Since it is limited to a single direction, the sum of the **sine of the longitude** <term>bhujajyā; the horizontal component of the planetary position</term> is at its maximum. Therefore, by the **cosine-gnomon** <term>koṭi-śaṅku</term> and the thread aligned with the sun, the multipliers for the northern direction are set down. These are established; then, in the region of seventy degrees, at the location of the arc, the reduction of the center occurs. The proper multiplier of the sine, whose multipliers are the lights... are the **cosines** <term>koṭi</term>... as stated in the previous opinion.

Its nature is not [merely] small. By three... the result in **fingers** <term>aṅgula; a traditional unit of measurement roughly equal to the width of a finger</term> is obtained. It is extended by the length of the **fingers**... with the width of a **finger**... or by another method it is well-known and should be done. Just as the ears... the face and the sides are bound with cloth and a wick.

**Here is the derivation:**
<note>original: *atropattiḥ*; this marks the beginning of the formal geometric proof common in Sanskrit scientific texts.</note>

When the **noon-sun** <term>madhyārka</term> is in the **southern hemisphere** <term>dakṣiṇa-gola</term>, the **gnomon** <term>śaṅku; a vertical pillar used to cast a shadow for measurement</term> is at its lowest. The **cosine** <term>koṭi</term> is the result-sine, and the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> is the distance from the eye. In this position, the calculation of the **minutes of arc** <term>kalā</term> is done. From the **radius** <term>trijyā; the total radius of the circle, usually taken as 3438 minutes in Indian trigonometry</term>, the minutes are calculated. Above the **cosine**, the **sine** is equal. That which is the **maximum gnomon** <term>parama-śaṅku</term> is the **hypotenuse**. The **radius** is the **hypotenuse**... the **cosine** is given here. In the calculation board, which represents the center, the measure is given in **fingers** <term>aṅgula</term> and other units.

It explains how to translate these celestial angles into physical measurements on the instrument using units like "fingers" (angulas).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical proof (atropattiḥ) regarding the geometry of the sun's position and the resulting shadows on the Tūryayantra (quadrant). It follows the previous page’s discussion on the southern hemisphere and the relationship between the sine of the sun's longitude and the gnomon.</meta>

<margin>Siddhānta-tattva-viveka <note>A famous 17th-century astronomical treatise by Kamalākara</note></margin>

From the given position, the surface of the base of the observation site should be known. Then, by the **Rule of Three** <term>traiśāśika; a mathematical proportion where the fourth term is found from three known values</term>, the result is obtained in one place. According to the method stated here:

The **sine** <term>jyā</term> <note>original: *sijyā/jyā*</note>, by the power of the calculation and divided by the result, becomes the **cosine** <term>koṭi</term>. That very sine is the result of the multiplication... divided by the sine, it proceeds as the form of the **sine of the colatitude** <term>lamba-jyā; the sine of the angle of the celestial equator above the horizon</term>. It is calculated by the difference of the **radius** <term>trijyā</term>, and the product of the **cosine** <term>koṭi</term> and the sine, divided by the radius, is subtracted from the result.

In this case, the sine is greater than the position... and the errors of the past are avoided. Here, the **declination** <term>krānti; the angular distance of a celestial body north or south of the celestial equator</term> is measured as described.

The **sine of the maximum declination** <term>parama-krānti-jyā</term> is 92193 <note>original: *paramakrāntijyā ९२१९३*</note>. Its **square** <term>varga</term> is 948150. Divided by the square of the **radius** <term>trijyā</term>, 15919, the **square root** <term>mūla</term> is 2712.25. From this, the **earth-sine** <term>kubhjyā; a specific sine value used in calculating the sun's rising and setting</term> is 935.

In this context, for the sake of the greater half, if more than a sixth is added, an **eclipse** <term>grahaṇa</term> may occur... The sine is established first... the maximum result... the measurement in **fingers** <term>aṅgula; a traditional unit of measurement</term> is 4.

**Here is the rationale:** <term>atropapattiḥ; a formal mathematical proof or logical derivation</term>

In the celestial **sphere** <term>gola</term>, at the end of the sign **Gemini** <term>mithunānta</term>, the **sine of the result** <term>phala-jyā</term> acts as the **cosine** <term>koṭi; the perpendicular side of a right-angled triangle</term>. The **sine of maximum declination** <term>parama-krānti-jyā; the sun's highest angle from the celestial equator</term> is the **base sine** <term>bhuja-jyā</term>, and the **radius** <term>trijyā</term> is the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>.

By observing this triangle of **declination** <term>krānti</term>, and applying the rule of three, one calculates the declination for one, two, or three signs starting from **Mars** <term>kuja</term> <note>original: "kujād"; likely referring to the intersection points or planetary longitude</note>. In the declination triangle, the **sine of declination** <term>krānti-jyā</term> is the base, the **sine of the day-radius** <term>dyu-jyā</term> is the cosine, and the **radius** <term>trijyā</term> is the hypotenuse.

Using these proportions, from the cosine of the day-radius and the radius, the calculation for the end of **Gemini** <note>original: "mithunānta"</note> is established. This represents the distance from the center to the **maximum declination** <term>parama-krānti</term>. By this calculation, the true position of the signs is known. Then, through the process of **ascensional difference** <term>cara-karma; the correction used to transform equatorial rising times into local ones</term>, the rising times for **Aries** and the following signs are positioned in the sky. With the **radius** as the hypotenuse, the parts of the zodiac signs are known... the calculation of the results follows this sequence...

<margin>15</margin>The results derived from the **Crest-jewel of Mathematical Astronomy** <term>Siddhānta Śiromaṇi; the 12th-century masterpiece by Bhāskara II</term> are established here through the sequence of hypotenuse and transformations. These values, beginning with **Aries** <term>meṣādi</term>, represent the actual manifested rising times.

At the desired time, starting from **Aries** <note>original: "meṣādi"; the first sign of the zodiac</note> and other signs, the **Ascendant** <term>Lagna; the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon</term> is determined. Whether it be day or night, the time is calculated by observing which sign is rising. Following the previously mentioned method, the calculation is performed.

<margin>15</margin> Through this process, one should calculate the **sixty-five parts** <note>original: "pañca-ṣaṣṭi-bhāge"</note> by considering the signs starting from Aries. The **rising times** <term>udaya-ghaṭī; the duration of a sign's ascent</term> are measured in units of **thirty ghatis** <note>one ghati is 24 minutes, making 30 ghatis equal to 12 hours</note> for every **six signs** <note>original: "ṣaḍ-rāśayaḥ"</note>. These values are then divided according to the mathematical system of the **Lagna-tantra** <note>a technical treatise on calculating the Ascendant</note>. By following the path of the twelve signs and subtracting the desired portions, the precise Ascendant is found.

When the **Serpent** <term>Sarpa; refers to Rahu or the presiding deity of the Ashlesha constellation</term> and the Ascendant are calculated, the difference between these two specific times is known as the **afflicted time** <term>duṣṭa-kāla; a period considered inauspicious or requiring correction in astronomical math</term>. This is the underlying proof for verse fifteen.

Regarding the constellation **Ashlesha** <note>original: "Aśleṣā"; the 9th lunar mansion, symbolized by a serpent</note>, it is said that the darkness or "stain" associated with the serpent is analyzed here. One must calculate the position of the Moon in relation to this, as described in the ancient texts. By determining the time elapsed in the day and the remaining time, the position is established.

<margin>16</margin> The refuge or position given to **Ashlesha** is like a form observed through a "cavity" or opening in the sky. Following the prescribed method, one calculates the time elapsed on that day. By taking the longitude of the **Serpent** and adding **six signs** <note>original: "ṣaḍ-sarpeṇa"; adding 180 degrees to find the point exactly opposite</note> to it, one enters into the calculation of the end of the night. If this value is greater than the total, it is added to the night-duration to find the true time elapsed since the **fall of night** <term>rātri-pāta</term>.

The calculation of the Serpent's position, when full or excessive, is thus used to determine the exact time passed during the night.

18. This is the **twelfth part** <note>original: "bhāga dvādaśo"; likely referring to a specific division of a circle or a step in a twelve-part proof</note>. In this diagram, the **sine of the correction** <term>phala-jyā; the trigonometric value used to calculate the difference between a planet's mean and true position</term> is derived from the **radius** <term>trijyā; the standard radius of 3438 minutes used in Indian trigonometry</term>. From the **anomaly** <term>kendra</term>, the sine of the arm is produced, which determines the diameter of the result.

The **circumference** <term>paridhi</term> is divided into twelve parts and illuminated as if by a lamp. Having placed these values, one should always calculate the proportion using the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> and the radius. This forms the **sine of the arm** <term>bhujajyā</term>, from which the desired result is obtained.

The **true sine** <note>original: "spaṣṭa-jyā"</note> is the distance from the zenith line to the planet. Here, the relationship between the **center of the eccentric circle** <note>original: "prativṛtta-garbha"</note> and the **center of the Earth** <term>bhū-garbha</term> is considered. The difference between these two centers is the result-sine, which is vital for the calculation.

In the **Chapter on True Positions** <note>original: "spaṣṭādhikāra"; a standard section in Siddhāntic texts dealing with the computation of true planetary longitudes</note>, the method for calculating the hypotenuse and the resulting sine has already been demonstrated. For the **Sun and Moon** <note>original: "sūrya-candramas"</note>, the correction is stated directly. For **Mars and the other planets** <note>original: "bhaumādya"</note>, it is found through a process of repeated calculation because the hypotenuse changes. By following these rows of calculations and removing the differences until they disappear, the mathematician reaches the true position. 18.

<margin>Sitāvi</margin> <note>Likely an abbreviation for a specific rule or author's name</note>

When the Sun is in a particular **zodiac sign** <term>rāśi</term>, the **lunar day** <term>tithi</term> is known as the "bright" <note>original: "sitā"; referring to the waxing phase or Śukla Paksha</note> tithi. The wise student of the Son of Arka <note>original: "aṅkaru-putrasya"; potentially referring to a lineage of astronomers or a specific planetary calculation</note> should know that the sum is decreased by a **tenth part** <term>daśabhāga</term>. When the lunar day is subtracted and the result is doubled, the Sun’s position is obtained. 18 21. Here, this is the definition of the nature of the calculation: it is known through the forms of the daily rotations. In ten ways, the **diameter** <term>vyāsa</term> is divided. After the remainder is settled, the Moon should be calculated <unclear>kṛpa</unclear>.

In sequence, the elapsed **lunar days** <term>tithi</term> are measured with their **fractional parts** <term>sāvayava</term>. On that day, if the rise occurs during the **bright fortnight** <term>śukla-pakṣa</term> or at moonrise during the night in the **dark fortnight** <term>kṛṣṇa-pakṣa</term>, the Moon is observed in the sky. At that time, the **lunar mansion** <term>nakṣatra</term> should be known. By knowing the interval between the lunar mansions, the specific value is understood. However, due to the variation in the arrangement of the lunar mansions in the sky, that value with its fractional parts is obtained by doubling and subtracting a tenth part. If the lunar day is subtracted, the remainder remains. Even so, by subtracting a tenth part and subtracting the lunar day, the lunar mansion is found through the Sun's position. This is the **rational proof** <term>upapatti</term>: a lunar day is the interval between the Sun and the Moon, while a lunar mansion <margin>Rama</margin> <note>The name of the scribe or commentator</note>

is the movement of the Moon alone. Here, by doubling the Moon's position and subtracting the interval, the remaining value is the **alignment** <term>yoga</term>. Thus, the alignment is proven by doubling the lunar mansion and subtracting the result. Is this not the divisor for the **minutes of arc** <term>kalā</term> in the calculation of the lunar day?

### Calculations for the Cycles and the Spheres

The value of **seven hundred and thirty** <note>original: "triṃśat-adhikā saptaśatī"</note> is decreased to six hundred, then **eight hundred** <note>original: "aṣṭaśatī"</note> is reduced by **one-tenth of its own value** <term>nija-daśāṃśona</term>, which results in **seven hundred and twenty** <note>original: "viṃśat-adhikā saptaśatī"</note>. In this way, when the divisor is one, the **lunar day** <term>tithi</term> becomes increased. Seven, along with its own tenth part, is subtracted from that. This represents the **interval between the Sun and the Moon** <note>original: "ravi-candra-antaraṃ"</note>. When the Moon and other factors are subtracted from the exterior, the remainder is what is left. Therefore, through the calculation of the Moon, the position of the **node** <term>sapāta</term> <note>Referring to Rāhu or Ketu, the points where the orbits of the Sun and Moon intersect</note> is brought near.

Here too, due to the difference in the divisor, after subtracting its own fraction, and as previously done with the lunar day, 19 is subtracted from the mean. The remainder is twenty. Thus, through the Moon's motion, the node is brought into proximity. 18. Having subtracted three from the **ascendant** <term>lagna</term>, when the Sun's heat is minimal, the **node** <term>surpāta</term> is touched by the **sine** <term>jīva</term>. From the node to the sine, starting from **Aries** <note>original: "meṣānna"</note>, the delay in the **auspicious moment** <term>muhūrta</term> is given.

Then, that which is the ninth is the knowledge of the **parallax** <term>nati</term>. Along with the node, the **latitude** <term>śara</term> <note>literally "arrow"; the celestial latitude or the perpendicular distance from the ecliptic</note> is 30. In the observation, through the **western ascendant** <note>original: "paścimona-lagnena"</note>, the Sun is drawn forth. From the birth of the Great Asura <note>original: "āsuraja"; likely a reference to the Sūrya Siddhānta, traditionally revealed by the Sun to the asura Maya</note>, the calculation is perfected in its proper sequence. Then, by placing the calculation in **Mars** <term>kuja</term>, and moving outward from the position of Mars, one observes the **Northern Sphere** <term>uttara-gola</term> and the **Southern Sphere** <term>dakṣiṇa-gola</term>.

By facing the direction of the **Kedra** <note>original: "kedābhimukha"; possibly a corruption of Kendra or a specific local directional term</note>, one should provide the **fingers of measure** <term>aṅgulāni</term> <note>a traditional unit of measurement roughly equal to the width of a finger</note>. Having become something significant, this remains the same on days such as the **vernal equinox** <note>original: "vaṣādī-dine"; the start of the year or equinox</note> when the shadow is equal. There, in the Southern Sphere, the central position is observed as being equal to the thread of the observation point.

### Geometric Proofs of the Nonagesimal and Parallax

In the calculation of the line-string within the **northern hemisphere** <term>uttara gola</term>, the knowledge of the seventh position is situated within this line. From that base horizontal line to the circumference, the **sine** <term>jya</term> <note>The mathematical function used to determine distances in the celestial sphere</note> is that which belongs to the seventh division of the **nonagesimal** <term>tribhona lagna</term> <note>The point of the ecliptic that is at the highest point above the horizon; literally "the ascendant minus three signs"</note>. Its tip is placed upon the circumference...

...not rotating, the visible part minus the **ascendant** <term>lagna</term>, the seventh [division] multiplied by the Sun. On the line equal to the nonagesimal, there is a variation; this is the meaning. In that visible form, which is characterized by the nonagesimal, the three steps of rotation are found. Upon touching the nonagesimal, the center position is reached. There, the measurement in **digits** <term>angula</term> <note>A traditional unit of length, roughly the width of a finger, used here for measuring coordinates on an instrument</note> is observed. In that observed place, the **sine** <term>jiva</term> is **eight hundred** <note>original: "aṣṭaśatiḥ"</note>. From the front portion of the circumference, having revealed the position of the division and having brought it there, the **declination** <term>kranti</term> is calculated, and the sine is found again.

From that point up to the sky, the **parallax** <term>lambana</term> <note>The displacement in the apparent position of a celestial body when viewed from different positions</note> occurs in degrees and other units. That which is to be calculated is achieved by multiplying by four. From the **node** <term>sapāta</term> <note>The intersection of the Moon's orbit with the ecliptic</note> and the Moon’s position, the **celestial latitude** <term>shara</term> <note>literally "arrow"; the angular distance of a planet north or south of the ecliptic</note> is determined in minutes of arc.

**The proof** <term>upapatti</term> is as follows: When the nonagesimal is equal [to the horizon] in the northern hemisphere...

### On the Calculation of the Meridian Distance

Now, concerning the sine of the altitude of the center of the nonagesimal, by which it is calculated: from the **meridian** <term>madhya</term>, the calculation of the degrees of the **zenith distance** <term>nata-nama-bhaga</term> leads to the determination of the **time units** <term>nadika</term> <note>Synonymous with ghatika; 1/60th of a day</note>. As stated by others, the time units are derived from the calculation of the degrees of the hour angle from the meridian through the sine of the altitude of the nonagesimal's center.

Thus it occurs: the sine of that nonagesimal center's altitude is used to prevent the loss of the multiplier and divisor <note>original: "gunahara-lopa," a mathematical step to simplify fractions by canceling terms</note>... it is as follows:

If, upon the **radius** <term>trijya</term> acting as the **hypotenuse** <term>karna</term>, there is the arc of the **sine of altitude** <term>unnata-jya</term>, then for the **cosine** <term>koti</term> of the nonagesimal, the result of the calculation is obtained. Similarly, at the tip of that sine, having placed it there again, with the radius-hypotenuse of 1 <note>The text specifies the radius value as '1' for the sake of the trigonometric ratio</note>, the result is the **side** <term>bhuja</term>...

### The Derivation of Celestial Latitude

What is the result? ...therefore, the result of nine times the minutes of latitude is proven. <unclear>sarpe 22</unclear>. Now, when the **rule of three** <term>trairashika</term> is applied, in the place of the standard...

...the calculation of the radius for the New Moon <term>darsha</term> is shown. He states the means for calculating the minutes: "The proof of the standard radius occurs when the node is at the center..."

...in the presence of the object, in the presence of the Moon, by that which is difficult to heat, not obstructed by the light, having gone to the nectar-rayed one <note>original: "sudhā-vã", the Moon</note> 21. Now, in some cases of **proportion** <term>anupāta</term>, the **Radius** <term>trijyā</term> <note>literally "the sine of three signs," representing the maximum sine or the total radius of the calculation circle</note> is the Standard. In that case, for the desired quantity—the **Requirement** <term>icchā</term> <note>the third term in the Rule of Three</note>—the measurement is taken from the **fingers** <term>angula</term> <note>a traditional unit of linear measurement used here for diagrammatic representation</note>...

...the calculation must be done. By this, it is to be calculated. Reaching until the point of union, the sine is the multiplier for the task. This is the meaning of the **Result of the Requirement** <term>icchā-phala</term>.

Or, for the Requirement established [at the zenith], multiplied by the fingers, it moves to the ascendant. Even there, the result would be the same. 

**The Proof** <term>upapatti</term> <note>the logical demonstration or derivation of a formula</note>:
In the proportion of the **Rule of Three** <term>trairāśika</term>, the **Radius** <term>trijyā</term> is the Standard <term>pramāṇa</term>. In that case, the Result of the Standard is like the multiplier. The Radius is that which produces the Result of the Requirement. There, by the occurrence of the Requirement, in the sky...

...having established [the values], so that there is **equality between the multiplier and the divisor** <term>guṇa-harayoḥ samatā</term>. In such a proportion: "If by the Radius, this result is obtained, then by the [sine of the] Requirement, what is obtained?"

### The Proof
<note>original: "athopapattiḥ", the formal demonstration of the mathematical logic</note>

After establishing the **standard measure** <term>pramāṇa</term> and the ratio, the **proportion** <term>anupāta</term> is calculated as follows: If, in the standard triangle, the **Base** <term>bhuja</term> is the known measure and the **Hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> is the **Radius** <term>trijyā</term>, then what is the value of the hypotenuse for the given base? The result obtained is then converted into the scale of the Radius. This result reaches the center point at the end of the sum of the **sine-terms** <term>jyā-pada</term>.

The proportion is structured thus: "If, using the **Radius** <term>trijyā</term> as the standard, this much is the shadow, then what is the result for the **desired shadow** <term>icchā-chāyā</term>?" By multiplying the total by the desired shadow and dividing by the Radius, the **Result of the Requirement** <term>icchā-phala</term> <note>the specific value the mathematician is trying to find</note> is produced.

Here too, because the results must remain consistent, the calculation is set up: If the Radius is the standard measure and this is the shadow, what is the result for the desired shadow? One must divide by the Radius to find the result. This result is then converted into the Radius-scale. Then, another proportion is made: If the Radius is the Hypotenuse and the desired value is the Base, then for this Radius-scaled value, what is the Hypotenuse? This result represents the distance reaching toward the **Line of the Sky** <note>original: "ākāśa-rekhā", likely referring to the vertical meridian or the horizon line</note> or the position of the Sine... <margin>Rama</margin>

...in the form of the Base, the **Result of the Requirement** <term>icchā-phala</term> is obtained as **23**. The **radius** <term>trijyā</term> <note>literally "the sine of three signs," the standard radius used in Indian trigonometry, often 3438 minutes of arc</note> multiplied by the **Sun's equation** <term>sūryaphala</term> and divided by the result gives the **desired rod** <term>phalaiṣyaṣṭi</term> <note>referring to the specific length of the gnomon or pointer for this calculation</note>. In whatever **plate** <term>phalaka</term> <note>the flat surface of the instrument</note> the five sines are fixed, that is the cause for the next operation. [3]

The operation of the results is just as the motion of the Sun is equal to the motion of the **ascendant** <term>lagna</term>. The **shadow** <term>chāyā</term> and the **brilliance** <term>tejas</term> <note>referring to the direct light of the Sun</note> are to be compared by the observer. 

The **wise one** <term>matimatā</term> should arrange the instrument so that in whichever part the Sun is located, its image is projected on the corresponding **back part** <term>pṛṣṭhabhāga</term>. This means that the brilliance of the solar disk <term>sūrya-bimba</term> moves according to the motion of the instrument. ### The Derivation of the Sun's Shadow

He now states the **derivation of the sun's shadow** <term>sūryacchāyopapatti</term>. At the time of **sunrise** <term>sūryodaya</term>, from the center point, the end of the shadow lies on the **meridian line** <term>dharekhā</term> <note>original: "dharekhā", literally 'earth-line', used here for the north-south or equatorial reference line</note> at the desired time. From that meridian line, gradually, the **shadows** <term>chāyā</term> are formed. On the circumference of the circle of the **instrument** <term>yantra</term>, the geometry is established. The shadow created there, on that meridian line, belongs to the position of the observer within the globe. For the purpose of establishing the horizon, because of the diameter, at sunrise—and by implication at midday—the sun is measured by the thread of the **gnomon** <term>kuru-ma-sūtra</term> <note>a measuring cord or plumb-line used in conjunction with the gnomon</note>.

<meta>This page continues the geometric proof regarding solar shadows, specifically focusing on the relationship between the center of the solar disk, the gnomon's tip, and the resulting shadow cast upon the ground.</meta>

<margin>White Disk <note>original: "sitā viḥ", likely an abbreviation for the bright or white disk of the sun</note></margin> Since the center of the **disk** <term>bimba</term> <note>the circular face of the sun or moon</note> is located at the position of the measure, that which passes through the center is the shadow. In that calculation, the intersection point of the **meridian line** <note>original: "dīrgha rekhā", literally 'long line'</note> and the **circumference** <term>paridhi</term> is the division. Between those two points, there is a single internal contact. Not at all times of observation, but at the time of the sun's elevation, the shadow is situated above the station of the observer.

<margin>27</margin> Because the measure of the shadow's path occurs below the circumference in the northern transit, the shadow of contact forms a **fish-shaped figure** <term>matsya</term> <note>a geometric construction made of two intersecting arcs, used in traditional Indian geometry to determine direction or bisect lines</note>. The use of the word "only" in this context is for the purpose of ruling out the possibility of the shadow being in the same direction as the sun. The **contact** <term>sparśa</term> <note>the point where the light ray first touches the gnomon</note> of the shadow of the disk is situated in the direction facing the sun.

The tip of the shadow is known as the "contact point." Just as there is a change of location from the point of contact over time, so too does the tip of the shadow move, due to the difference in the rotation of the two. This is explained in verse 43. The position of the **gnomon** <term>śaṅku</term> <note>the vertical pillar or stick used to measure time by its shadow</note> provides the knowledge of the shadow's existence. The shadow is the measure on the earth-line.

The various parts or degrees on the horizon are the infinite parts. Among them, the light of the gnomon should be measured in parts. # Determination of the Directions via the Shadow

On the two sides where the **shadows** <term>chāyā</term> are formed, if there is a disturbance <note>likely referring to an irregularity in the surface or measurement</note> in the middle or outside, then at that place... during the day, the shadow follows the **meridian line** <term>sama-sūtra</term> <note>original: "sama-sūtra"; literally "even thread," referring to the straight line used to determine the east-west or north-south axes</note>. Between these two points, one determines the **South** <term>dakṣiṇa</term> and the **North** <term>uttara</term> through the prescribed procedure.

By knowing the measurement of the shadow’s form... to overcome the rigidity <note>or "slowness" (jāḍya) of the calculation</note> of the shadow’s tip, one should apply the inverse calculation. 

### The Mathematical Operation

The **equinoctial shadow** <note>original: "desha-akṣabhā"; the shadow cast by a vertical gnomon at noon on the day of the equinox</note> is multiplied by the **radius-sine** <term>trijyā</term> <note>The sine of the quadrant, a fundamental constant in Indian trigonometry</note> and then **divided by twelve** <term>dvādaśa-bhaktā</term> <note>The standard gnomon in Indian astronomy is 12 digits (angulas) tall, making this a conversion factor</note>. The **result** <term>phala</term> obtained from this calculation is then applied to the previously established North and South positions. 

By this result, the North and South directions are sequentially adjusted. Again, the equinoctial shadow is multiplied by the radius and divided by twelve to find the result. With that result, the previous positions are corrected... joined with the calculated value. 

Then, for the **gnomon** <term>śaṅku</term>, using the previously known measurement, that value is multiplied and divided by the radius. This result allows for the **knowledge of the directions** <term>dig-jñāna</term> <note>The science of orienting oneself and instruments toward the true cardinal points</note> even as the sun moves. 

As previously explained regarding the **visible circle** <term>dṛśya-cakra</term>, the statement concerning the knowledge of directions through the shadow is vital. When the shadow joins toward the South, the result is subtracted from the North; when it moves toward the North, it is added... For a **gnomon measured at sixty** <term>ṣaṣṭi-mita-śaṅkoḥ</term> <note>While 12 is common, 60 units is used for higher precision in sexagesimal (base-60) systems</note>, the **base** <term>bhuja</term> <note>The horizontal side of the right-angled triangle formed by the gnomon and its shadow</note> is calculated as follows:

### The Rule of Three

This is determined by the **Rule of Three** <term>trairāśika</term> <note>a mathematical proportion: if A results in B, what does C result in?</note>. That is sufficient for the earth.

<margin>Titā Vi 32</margin>
Regarding the three **gnomons** <term>shaṅku</term> <note>original: "tisra-shaṅku"; likely referring to three specific positions or types of gnomon measurements</note> at night, when the temporal calculation is performed through the **water-level instrument** <term>udapāla</term> <note>original: "udapāla-kālikā"; an instrument used to establish a perfectly horizontal plane for astronomical measurements</note>, even if there is a difference in the results of the calculation, there is no error in the center of the time-frame. Because the star **Agastya** <term>Agastya</term> <note>The star Canopus, which is vital in Indian astronomy for southern orientation and determining latitude</note> is present there, even if there is ignorance of the exact time, the calculation related to it remains valid. For one who knows the ten stars <unclear>sa</unclear>, there is no fault in the measurement. This is as previously described.

The distance between the **radius** <term>trijyā</term> <note>original: "trijyā"; the total sine of the arc, typically the radius of the circle used in calculations</note> and the other **angle** <term>koṇa</term> is marked by the death-line <note>original: "maraṇa-rekhā"; likely a technical term for a specific limit or intersecting line</note> and the cord of the horizon instrument. Even so, there is a mixture of the horizon and the temporal sine. Beneath that, the cord of the horizon instrument is like a plumb-line. In this expansion, the cord follows the **day-night cycle** <term>ahorātra</term>. By following the movement of the celestial bodies in this manner, the statement is simply a **demonstration of the essential nature** <term>svarūpa-mātropapatti</term> <note>A mathematical proof that illustrates the inherent properties of the geometric figure being studied</note> of the position, rather than a calculation of time itself. This is the intention of verse 69.

The authors state the reason for providing this specific demonstration of the source material: through the opinions known as the **horizon-measure** <term>udapāla-mata</term>, if the **Sun** <term>Ravi</term> is not visible, a nearby substitute is used. If this is divided by **sixty** <note>referring to the 60 ghatikas that make up a full day</note>, the elapsed time becomes clear.

The difference between the north and the south is the nature of the **gnomon’s shadow-base** <term>shaṅku-tala</term> <note>the distance from the center of the gnomon to the rising/setting line of the sun</note>. In the gnomon's angle, this is divided by the **base of twelve** <term>dvādaśa-koṭi</term> <note>the standard height of a gnomon in Indian astronomy is twelve units; the shadow triangle is calculated based on this constant</note>. <meta>This page continues the technical mathematical derivation of astronomical measurements, specifically the calculation of time using the shadow cast by a gnomon.
