For that suspended instrument, if the shadow falls in an unintended place, then from the beginning of the hole to the tip of the shadow, how many **ghatis** <term>ghaṭī</term> <note>a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> have passed since midday? Or how many **ghatis** are remaining in the afternoon? 

Even if this is the case, if the tip of the shadow falls on the staff <term>yaṣṭi</term> <note>the measuring rod or pole</note>, what is the calculation from that corner in the morning? If the shadow is preceded by the previously stated sequence of time, it belongs to the sun. The fingers <term>aṅgula</term> <note>a standard unit of measure, roughly a finger's width</note> measured from the tip of the shadow until its depletion are the basis of knowledge, if the time sequence has been stated.

With the observations of men, for the desired fingers, why are the results endowed with the previous heights? **69** <note>This likely refers to verse 69 of the chapter being commented upon.</note> By the **sine of zenith distance** <term>dṛg-jyā</term> <note>original: "dṛg-jyā"; the sine of the angular distance from the zenith to the celestial body</note> multiplied by the sun and divided by the **gnomon** <term>śaṅku</term> <note>the vertical pillar of a sundial</note>, thus for the months starting with **Aries** <term>meṣādi</term> and so on, the expert knows the external deduction. He acknowledges the **rationale** <term>upapatti</term> <note>original: "opapatti" (a phonetic variant of upapatti); a formal proof or logical derivation in Indian mathematics</note> regarding this.

This rationale is called the **Rationale of the Surya Siddhanta** <term>sūrya-siddhāntopapatti</term> <note>The Surya Siddhanta is one of the foundational Sanskrit treatises on Indian astronomy</note>. This means it is the implicit rationale of this specific calculation.

He states the proposed rationale: The rod touching the ground is divided into three parts. In the appearance of the line of light, within the middle of half a unit, the remaining shadow is known in fingers. By digging the base, the rod becomes equal to the sun’s measurement. Therefore, from all of this, the **gnomon** is produced. Because of its orientation, the tip of the shadow is determined accordingly.

Just as the sun ascends higher, so the shadow **[diminishes]** <note>The OCR text 'पोयपीपने' appears to be a transcription error for a verb describing the shadow's reduction</note>. When the sun reaches the meridian <note>midday</note>, the gnomon <term>śaṅku</term> <note>the vertical pillar of a sundial</note> also acts accordingly. For this reason, because the sun is at its peak, the gnomon's divisor is absent <note>meaning the shadow is at its shortest or non-existent at the zenith</note>, and the maximum altitude is achieved. Thus, due to the extreme similarity of the gnomon at midday, there is an absence of the **result** <term>phala</term> <note>the mathematical outcome of the shadow calculation</note>. In this context, the gnomon is characterized as a "visual gnomon" <term>dṛk-śaṅku</term>.

Now, in the opposite manner and following the northern way, he describes his own **imaginary circle** <term>kalpita cakra</term> <note>the geometric construction or the physical disk of the instrument</note>. As described by the experts, the circle <term>cakra</term> should be shaped such that its center is uniform and the outer part is even.

**71**

Thus, as previously stated, the horizontal line is divided; there are twelve parts of the multiplier—what is that? From the first <term>nāḍikā</term> <note>synonym for ghaṭī</note> onwards, the sun’s gnomon measurement is known in the form of fingers. 

This means that at the center of one’s own sheet: the sine of the zenith distance <note>original: "natāṃśa-jyā"; the angular distance from the point directly overhead</note> at the desired time is multiplied by twelve <note>the standard height of the gnomon</note> and divided by the sine of the altitude. That result is the desired "hour-angle" time <term>nata-kāla</term> expressed in fingers. This addresses the question: "What is the sun’s gnomon-shadow starting from the first and subsequent hours?" On the sheet representing the day's length, one should calculate the number of fingers for each <term>ghaṭikā</term> and mark the hour-points accordingly.

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical derivation of the instrument's scales, following the previous discussion on the geometry of the shadow and the 12-digit gnomon. In the difference, having the form of a versine <term>śara</term> <note>original: "śara-rūpa"; literally 'arrow-shaped,' referring to the vertical segment of an arc</note>... **76** Here, at the intersection of the equatorial markings, the base <term>bhuja</term> <note>the horizontal component or sine-related distance</note> is to be calculated for each time-unit <term>ghaṭikā</term> <note>a unit of 24 minutes</note> of the time elapsed since sunrise <term>unnata-kāla</term>. Through the method of the observed circumference, and by imagining the motion at the end of the day, the base <term>bhuja</term> should be established for the time-units <term>nāḍī</term> <note>synonym for ghaṭikā</note>. From the positions of the versines, through repeated practice, the two intersection points on the region of the arc's circumference are to be known. Then, after calculating the positive additive value <term>dhana</term> for that day, the base of the imagined full arc within that day's duration should be constructed. **77**

### Calculation of the Base

He now states the method for calculating the base <term>bhuja-sādhana</term>:

The desired gnomon <term>iṣṭa-śaṅku</term> <note>the altitude of the sun expressed as the height of a vertical rod</note> obtained through calculation is multiplied by the equinoctial shadow <term>pala-prabhā</term> <note>the shadow cast at noon on the equinox, which indicates the observer's latitude</note> and divided by twelve <note>original: "arka"; the Sun, used here as a numerical symbol for 12</note>. If the directions are the same, they are added; if they are different, they are subtracted. This result, when adjusted for the specific direction, shall be the base <term>bhuja</term>. **98**

This is established within the instrument. Following that, he speaks of the knowledge of the time-units elapsed since sunrise <term>unnata-ghaṭī</term> by means of the six zodiac signs <term>rāśi</term>. This is understood from the measure of the sines <term>jyā</term> of the zodiac signs...

# Calculations for the Gnomon and Midday Line

If the expert of the region <note>the observer or surveyor</note> [determines] the north-south <term>dakṣiṇottara</term> line of the two regions, which originates from the equal directions of the sun <note>original: "somajaḥ", likely referring to solar positions</note>, then the direction in the previous [calculation] is otherwise. That line will then become the segment of the midday line in the desired direction. # The Water Clock and the External Instrument

I shall thereafter speak [of the measurement]. By the measure prescribed by the masters, how long is a <term>ghaṭikā</term> <note>a traditional Indian unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note>? If the vessel is made according to a single standard, then the smaller or larger [vessels] are produced by the same ratio. This is called the <term>ghaṭī-pātra</term> <note>original: "ghatī-pātra"; the bowl or vessel of a water-clock, which has a small hole to allow water to enter at a fixed rate</note>. This presentation serves as the mathematical proof (84).

Whether positioned on the earth, in the water, or in the sky, it remains steady like a divine image. While its precise measurement is not stated here, its height is known by observing the Sun <note>original: "āditya-darśana"</note>. For the one standing there, the knowledge of the location and its ratio is like a jewel of knowledge. Here, the wood-based instrument <note>original: "vṛkṣātmaka yantra"</note> is the primary means for resolving all doubts regarding the essential work. I shall describe it (85).

# The Proportions of the Sighting Instrument

It should be understood by the wise that for the whole [system of instruments], the following are the limits of measurement. There are six standards of measurement established at the tip and at the base <note>original: "agra-mūlayoḥ"; referring to the top and bottom of a gnomon or sighting pole</note>. Among these, there is a mutual reliance on a single standard. Such is the insight produced regarding the construction of the instrument and the technical terms used therein. 

Whether observing between the stars or independently, one should observe the six-fold division situated at the base. This specific form in the six-fold division is distinct. One part of this serves as the distance between the instrument and the point of observation. 

In the middle of the six measures, the <term>bhuja</term> <note>original: "bhuja"; the horizontal side or "arm" of a right-angled triangle in Indian geometry</note> is established. The distance between the measurements of the instrument is thus recorded. In the context of this specific observation, the <term>koṭi</term> <note>original: "koṭi"; the vertical side or perpendicular of a right-angled triangle</note> is oriented toward the west. By these calculations and through the geometric figures <note>original: "kṣetra"; refers here to the triangles generated by the sighting lines</note> produced by the instrument, one calculates using the six-fold measure. 

The <term>bhuja</term> or horizontal base is sixty units, extending to the region of the instrument. In the upper part of the instrument, the <term>koṭi</term> (perpendicular) is defined. This geometric field applies to everything, whether in the "upper instrument" or in the "lower instrument" <note>likely referring to the sighting tube and the water clock respectively</note>. 

From this, the nature of this class of instruments is known and reasoned out by the wise. For those who are enlightened, this is the measurement of the entire collection of instruments (87). This is achieved through the observation of the <term>Tantra-meru</term> <note>literally "The Axis of the System," likely referring to the primary vertical pole or the central mathematical principle of the treatise</note> and the fall [of the shadow].

# Calculations of Altitude and Zenith Distance

From the sine of the zenith distance and the sine of the altitude <note>original: "natāṃśa-unnatāṃśa-jyā"; the angular distances from the zenith and the horizon respectively</note>, one obtains the knowledge of the **hour angle** <note>original: "nata kāla"; the time elapsed since a celestial body was at the local meridian</note>. From this calculation, the <term>bhuja</term> (base), the <term>koṭi</term> (perpendicular), and the <term>trijyā</term> (radius) are divided by sixty; the resulting values are obtained in order. The sine of the zenith distance multiplied by... [is used] in the calculation of sixty. This is the established method.

By using the perpendicular and the radius in the calculation, and through the intersection of these two, the value known as the <term>śanku</term> <note>original: "śaṅku"; the gnomon or vertical pillar used to cast a shadow for measurement</note> is to be determined. The altitude is thus calculated. For the knowledge of the zenith distance, the same method is applied in reverse through the arc. 

### Observation of the Pole Star

One should observe the **Pole Star** <note>original: "dhruva-tārā"</note> using the aforementioned method. By multiplying the altitude and the radius, and through the resulting values, the same process is repeated. In the geometric figure <note>original: "kṣetra"</note> used for observing the Pole Star, the <term>koṭi</term> (perpendicular) is fixed at **twelve units**. 

By dividing the resulting figure, one achieves the result. Again, by the position of the celestial body and the method previously described, the zenith distance and altitude sines are calculated. From these sines, the twelve-unit perpendicular and the <term>bhuja</term> (base) are determined. 

Through this intersection, the established calculation for the <term>akṣāṃśa</term> <note>original: "akṣāṃśa"; the terrestrial latitude of the observer</note> is produced. This is based on the proportion of the radius and the sixty-unit standard. <margin>M</margin> Through the zenith distance, the latitude is obtained. At the base of the gnomon's thread, the result is seen. 

Alternatively, if the perpendicular is twelve units, the calculation for the **gnomon's shadow** is perfected. When the perpendicular is successfully established through observation, then by the measure of twelve, the <term>palabhā</term> <note>original: "palabhā"; the equinoctial midday shadow, used to determine the latitude of a specific location</note> is produced. Thus, the perpendicular...

# Calculations for Distance and Height of Observed Objects

By the method known as the **arc-fall calculation** <note>original: "dhanupāta"; a trigonometric method for determining the intersection of arcs or sine values</note>, the calculator should establish the positions of the two **viewing apertures** <note>original: "vaktra"; the openings or 'mouths' of the sighting instrument</note> [82]. Here, the measurement of the ground distance between the observer’s sighting position and the object is stated. According to the sighting method using a **bamboo pole** <note>original: "vaṃśa"; a standard reference object in Indian surveying, used here to demonstrate height and distance calculations</note> [86]:

The product of the **base** <term>bhujā</term> and the desired arc, divided by the **perpendicular** <term>koṭi</term>, yields the ground distance to the entrance point of the sighting, and so on. This refers to the measurement from the base of the bamboo pole to the end of the gnomon, including the vertical heights. From the sighting of the root (the base), looking upward to the visible portion... <unclear>na jo na podyatiḥ</unclear>. When divided by the sighting perpendicular, the result is the location of the entry point. The distance between these two points is the true ground measurement.

### The Geometrical Proof

Here is the **geometrical proof** <note>original: "upapatti"; the logical derivation or demonstration of the mathematical rule</note>: 
The desired arc acts as the perpendicular, and the difference between the observer's station and the aperture's position is the base. The visual line from the eye through the aperture forms the **hypotenuse** <note>original: "karṇa"</note>. According to the sighting of the root perpendicular and the sighting of the root base [86], the form produced by the **arc-fall** from the desired arc perpendicular is established [80].

Now, the measurement of the height of the bamboo pole and other objects is explained: 
The ground measurement, multiplied by the upper perpendicular of the sighting of the bamboo tip, and then divided by... the result obtained is the **length of the bamboo**. For a precise sighting of the tip of the bamboo pole, the ground distance between the two apertures is multiplied by the given perpendicular and divided by the base produced by that sighting. The result is the height/elevation of the bamboo.

**The Proof:** 
Using a **horizontal level-line** <note>original: "sama-sūtra"; a perfectly horizontal reference line used in construction and astronomy</note>, the portion of the bamboo visible above that line constitutes the perpendicular in that section...

# Calculating Heights and Distances from the Tip of a Bamboo Pole

From the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> formed by the **line of sight** <term>dṛksūtra</term> to the tip of the bamboo, the **base** <term>bhujā</term> is equal to the earth <note>original: "mṛttulyabhujaḥ"; likely implying the horizontal ground distance</note>. In the calculation of field measurement, what is the result when the **perpendicular of the sighting** <term>vedha-koṭi</term> is related to the base?

The tip of the line of sight is joined with the distance from the base of the bamboo or a tree [91]. Now, the author introduces the method for determining the **ground measurement** <term>bhūmāna</term> based on sightings taken from one’s own position:

"Now, from the ground measurements determined by sightings of the tip, starting from the bamboo..."

Establish the ground measurement between the two points. The value produced from the root of the bamboo... <unclear>ka</unclear>... is found by means of two sightings of the tip. This refers to the instruction to use "two sightings of the tip" <note>original: "agravedhābhyām"</note>. This method is used to eliminate the errors associated with a single, small-scale sighting. This is what should be stated regarding the location of the bamboo.

...the measurement of the middle ground is obtained. This is the cause for knowing the dimensions of the bamboo and other such objects [92]. To fulfill what was promised, he states the method for an observer who is **seated** <note>original: "upaviṣṭhena"</note>:

The base and the perpendicular should be known. Even for an observer standing upright, the base and perpendicular are known from the sighting of the tip of the bamboo [93].

The perpendicular divided by its own base... [94]. When these two sightings are established on the ground... the wise practitioner, remaining in his own position, should determine the base and perpendicular from the sighting of the bamboo tip. Here, while in his own position, or even if standing higher...

# Proofs of the Calculations for the Bamboo Pole

From the **sighting of the tip of the bamboo** <note>original: "vaṃśāgra-vedha"</note>, the **vertical sides** <term>koṭi</term> and the **horizontal sides** <term>bhujā</term> are established. When each vertical side is divided by its own corresponding horizontal side, a result is obtained [6]. By the difference of these results, the calculation is furthered. Therefore, from these values alone, the relationship between the horizontal and vertical sides determines the height of the bamboo [84].

Regarding the **rationalization of this proof** <term>upapatti</term>, for those who understand such things, the author speaks of the similarity between this geometric approach and the **method of single-variable algebra** <term>eka-varṇa-bīja-rīti</term>. 

Now follows the formal proof: 

In this context, the measurement of the bamboo and the measurement of the ground are the given proportions. The nature of the sighting is expressed through these proportions [85]. 

The vertical side here is the **base** <term>bhujā</term> multiplied by the bamboo’s height. In this method of determining the measurement, there is no difference between the height of the bamboo and the result of the calculation. In this specific method of derivation, the proof follows the rules of **algebraic analysis** <term>bīja</term>. 

Standing at a distance from the root of a straight bamboo pole, the ground measurement between the two points is established. The result of the sighting is then determined from the vertical and horizontal sides of the observer's position. What is accomplished by these sightings regarding the actual height of the bamboo? To answer this, he says: "From your horizontal side..." [86].

The vertical side is divided by the horizontal side... <unclear>bhaktā</unclear>...

It highlights the use of ratios between vertical and horizontal measurements and notes that the logic used is identical to single-variable algebraic methods.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical proof regarding the "bamboo pole problem." Having established the geometric side-ratios on the previous page, the text now transitions into the algebraic formalization (Bīja-gaṇita) required to solve for the unknown height, specifically referencing the methods of the 12th-century mathematician Bhaskara II.</meta>

<page-num>49</page-num>
<margin>5</margin>

In the process of **division** <term>bhajana</term>, it is said that when multiplying by small components, if we assume a sixth part, it retains the nature of the bamboo's own form. Thus, in the regional measurement, the inherent form is established as being of a single measure in both cases. He states the reason for this: because in the nature of the bamboo, the **fractions** <term>kalā</term> and their standards are identical to their respective divisors, there is no difference between them [81].

Regarding the purpose of the **two sides of the equation** <term>paksha</term>, he explains the "balancing of sides" <note>literally "the sides jump towards each other," referring to transposition</note>. Just as when two sides are equal, the **formal statement** <term>nyāsa</term> is made for the sake of **clearing the equation** <term>samashodhana</term> <note>the algebraic process of subtracting equal terms from both sides to isolate the unknown</note>. 

The notation is: **Un. 2 Mult. Ph. 1 Mult. 6 1** <note>original: "अ २ म फ १ म ६ १"; this is a shorthand algebraic layout where 'अ' (a) stands for 'avyakta' or the unknown variable</note>. This is provided as an illustration of the **horizontal side** <term>bhujā</term> and the **vertical side** <term>koṭi</term>. The intent is that the very essence of the two sides is for the purpose of this clearing.

As to how this clearing is performed: Following the **verse on clearing equations** <term>samashodhana-shloka</term> stated by **Bhaskara** in his work on **squares** <term>varga</term> <note>likely referring to the *Bīja-gaṇita* of Bhaskara II</note>, the result is derived. <unclear>The method of removing the middle term</unclear> is described here. For the sake of providing an example, Bhaskara himself has given an example in verse: 

"One should clear the other side and so on..." <note>original: "shodhaye-danya-pakshādi"</note>. By this, it is meant that once the values are determined, the base of that ground is attained. For the sake of instruction, this objective was stated by Bhaskara in his own treatise. In the verse, "one should clear the other side," it means moving terms to the other side. When there is no **additive** <term>kshepa</term>, the remaining values on their respective sides represent the solution.

<vocab>Bhaskara: Bhaskara II, the celebrated Indian mathematician; Samashodhana: The algebraic process of clearing or balancing an equation; Nyasa: The formal writing down or "layout" of a mathematical problem; Paksha: A side of an equation; Udaharana: An example or illustration; Ganita: Mathematics or calculation.</vocab>

By whatever objective the remaining manifest measurement is known, specifically regarding the **Sign of Capricorn** <term>Makara Rāśi</term> <note>the tenth sign of the zodiac, marking the winter solstice in ancient Indian astronomy</note>, the other **side of the equation** <term>pakṣa</term> is established in this same manner. In the intermediate space, for those [points or stars] situated in the middle of their own positions at night, the names are mentioned for the purpose of **division** <term>bhajana</term>. By the method described, being devoid of that difference, the upward result is established. This meaning is logically proven.

Now, following this, for the sake of easy understanding regarding the method of **calculation** <term>ānayana</term>, and in order to fully comprehend this, the verses spoken by the great teacher **Bhaskaracharya** in his own treatise, the *Crown of Treatises* <term>Siddhānta Shiromaṇi</term>, specifically in the **Chapter on Instruments** <term>Yantrādhyāya</term> where the verses are proclaimed <gloss>87</gloss>, he [Munishvara] provides an explanation of them, saying "by me" <gloss>88</gloss>.

He says: "If the remainder of the sixth part stays above... through the observation of the tip... the **horizontal arm** <term>bhujā</term> <note>the base of a right-angled triangle in Indian trigonometry</note> is the sixth part..." This serves as an **illustration** <term>uddeśaka</term> for the purpose of providing a practical example. "O friend, the **multiplier** <term>guṇa</term> is 8... without division... likewise in the observation of the two..." <note>The text here refers to 'vedha', which means an observation, sighting, or 'piercing' through an instrument's sight-hole</note>. Through the increase of one's own entry, the observation is known. 

In the "small side" <term>alpa-pakṣa</term> argument, for the purpose of understanding the observation... therefore, when the observation mentioned in one side is required, the remaining part is clearly explained in the **Marichi** <term>Marīcī</term> <note>literally "Ray of Light," the name of this specific commentary</note>. Through the context of these eight verses, the entry-measurement is determined.

# 75

<meta>Ink bleed-through from the back of the page is visible on the left margin.</meta>

He asks: what is this **five-fold nature** <term>svapañcarūpa</term>? Is this five-fold knowledge, which expresses the unity and connection between the principles of "Thou" and "That," <note>The author uses the Vedantic philosophical phrase "Tat Tvam Asi" (Thou Art That) as a metaphor for the perfect structural identity and mechanical unity of the machine</note> like a brilliant light born of the sphere?

It is said that the means of achieving it cannot be described; therefore, the knowledge of the **self-moving machine** <term>svayaṃvaha</term> and its five-fold form has been described by all in the following way.

**Bhāskarācārya** <note>The famous 12th-century Indian mathematician and astronomer</note> and others stated that such a thing cannot arise from nothing <note>original: "nāsataḥ," referring to the philosophical principle that something cannot be produced from non-existence</note> within its own class. Here, in this book titled **The Universal Emperor of Doctrines** <term>Siddhāntasārvabhauma</term>, I have stated the reason: 

A **glass jar** <term>kācaghaṭa</term> is a machine that uses mercury, water, sugar, and other helpful materials; in a new way, enclosed within a sphere, the desired form is produced. Thus, in the division of mathematics, the solution for **spherical mathematics** <term>golagaṇita</term> is obtained through this achievement. Having attained this, one can later resolve the issues of the **wheel-mechanism** <term>cakriyaka</term> which is delightful, and afterward, the feeling of wonder <term>camatkāra</term> regarding the calculation of a stable solar day is produced.

This was stated by the teacher Bhāskara: "In this way, by many different methods and through the science of many sights, they become one; this is the fruit of my work, which is free from the stain of error." Now, one might ask: if that is so, should only what was spoken by Bhāskara be followed?

To this he replies: In the cities, the knowledge of the **Europeans** <term>phirangijānā</term> <note>original: "phirangijānā," referring to the 'Franks' or Westerners, likely Portuguese or British clockmakers</note> is spoken of. Because ordinary men are unable to master their methods, their knowledge of the five-fold form—even when using mercury and water—does not result in a successful application or the completion of the instrument.

<meta>This page continues the technical and dialectical commentary of Munīśvara’s "Essence of the Truths of the Doctrines" (Siddhāntatattvaviveka). It moves from the general defense of his astronomical expertise to a specific physical description of solar phenomena during an eclipse, followed by a logical defense of his pedagogical method regarding "questions and answers."</meta>

### On the Appearance of the Solar Disc

If there were such light that appeared dull <term>malina</term>, then in the sky, it would resemble the dark body of a serpent; or rather, because of the excess [of darkness], the stars would appear dull or become impossible to see. Even so, the appearance of the **solar disc** <term>sūrya-bimba</term> <note>original: "sūrya-bimbopama," meaning comparable to the sun's circular plate</note> is described. In a **total eclipse** <term>sarva-grahaṇa</term>, the meaning is that the color is **black** <term>kṛṣṇa</term>, like the color of a serpent’s body <note>This likely refers to the dark silhouette of the Moon or the mythological shadow-planet Rāhu covering the Sun</note>.

By the very force of this [description], the colors are explained. Here, in the same way, the various facets of the "crushing" or occlusion <term>upamardaka</term> <note>referring to the Moon gradually covering the Sun</note> are considered. Regarding the appearance of these colors, the intention is to show that through the flashing forth of the answer <term>uttara-sphuraṇa</term>, the previous [erroneous] notion is removed. 

It is not the case [as the opponent says], because that would lead to the loss of the established and famous form of the phenomenon. Therefore, regarding the cause of satisfaction, he addresses the **question** <term>praśna</term>.

### On Questions and Answers

Regarding the phrase "causes for the satisfaction of a child" <note>original: "śiśu-toṣa-hetu," an idiom referring to simplified explanations meant for beginners or those with less expertise</note>, this is a specific attribute of the cause. An objection is raised: "Surely, there is an absence of answers to these questions?" 

In response, he says: "I do not provide answers to the aforementioned questions." The sentiment here is that the questions themselves are **fruitless** <term>vaiphalyena</term>. 

One might then argue: "But surely it is not appropriate to neglect the definition of that [subject] simply because of a lack of interest?" Otherwise, what would be the purpose of stating the answer later on? To this, he says... [it will be explained] in the following sections. In the previous part of the text, the beginning of the understanding... for the destruction of the greater form...

<summary>Munīśvara explains the visual "blackness" of a total solar eclipse by comparing it to the dark body of a serpent and then defends his refusal to answer certain "useless" elementary questions in his technical treatise.</summary>

<keywords>solar disc, total eclipse, occlusion, blackness, astronomy, methodology, Munīśvara, logic, solar appearance</keywords>

->**Siddhā-Vi**<-

<margin>Siddhā-Vi</margin>
<margin>46</margin>
The formulation of these concepts produces a certain difficulty <term>duḥkha-janaka</term>; the effort involved in this construction is the cause of one's own exhaustion. It is not that there is no capacity for those who follow the path of the traditional texts. Rather, the purpose of demonstrating the **new question and answer format** <term>navīna-praśnottara-rūpa</term> is to show the strength of this method, which remains unacknowledged by others even in the established systems. This is intended for those who are uninformed <term>anabhijñā</term>, as the proper understanding of the process cannot otherwise be achieved. This occurs due to the sequence of the context. 

Now, one might ask: "Will there be further definitions?" To this, he provides a specific description starting with the word **Darkness** <term>tamas</term> <note>original: "tamaso-tyādi," referring to the shadow or the mythological node Rāhu</note>. 

The face of the supreme serpent <note>Referring to Rāhu, the shadow planet that "swallows" the sun</note>, once it is released from the covering <term>nirmukta</term>, appears like a vessel of light. The idea is that it then possesses the capacity to fulfill the desires of those observing it.

### On the Specific Questions regarding Mean Motion

Regarding the subject of the **Chapter on Mean Motion** <term>madhyamādhikāra</term>, he presents specific questions: 

"By the force of the remainder of the **intercalary months** <term>adhimāsa-śeṣa</term> <note>The fractional part left over when calculating the number of extra months needed to align lunar and solar years</note>, find the **sum of days** <term>ahargaṇa</term> <note>literally "heap of days," the total number of days elapsed since the beginning of a cosmic epoch</note>. Also, from the remainder of the **omitted lunar days** <term>avama-śeṣa</term>... <unclear>nad-amau</unclear>... the sound is heard. When these are equal to the master's own calculations, surely a group of seventeen... <unclear>sapta-daśa-saṅgha</unclear>... in the calculation of the moon, it is said by the expert." 

<margin>Rāma</margin>
Thus, it is stated by the wise. From the remainder of the intercalary months, one must calculate the sum of days. All of this—the intercalary months, the remaining portions, and the resulting remainder—is for the sake of the **Sun** <term>sūrya</term>. When two sums of days have passed, and one is without a known sum of days, how is it determined? By the force of the remainder of the intercalary months, the calculation of the sum of days is achieved... 

<summary>Munīśvara explains that his "question and answer" method is necessary to clarify complex astronomical truths for the uninitiated, then presents a technical challenge: calculating the total elapsed days (Ahargaṇa) using only the remainders of intercalary months.</summary>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 46 of the "Essence of the Truths of the Doctrines" (Siddhāntatattvaviveka) by Munīśvara. Having established the pedagogical necessity of his "question and answer" style, he now dives into the complex mathematics of the "Mean Motion" (Madhyamādhikāra) chapter. Specifically, he is discussing the "Kuhaka" or mathematical riddles used to find the positions of the Sun and Moon using only the remainders of cycles, bypassing the need for a full count of elapsed days.</meta>

In this context, the term "remainderless" refers specifically to the **remainder of the intercalary months** <term>adhimāsa-śeṣa</term> <note>The fractional part of the lunar month that accumulates to eventually form an extra, intercalary month to keep the lunar and solar calendars aligned.</note>. From that remainder, the positions of the **Sun and Moon** <term>sūryacandrau</term> can be determined. Even without calculating the total **Count of Days** <term>ahargaṇa</term> <note>original: "vinā hargaṇa-sādhanaṃ"; Ahargana is the total number of days elapsed from the beginning of the current cosmic epoch (the Kalpa or Kali Yuga), which is the standard starting point for Indian astronomical calculations.</note>, a wise person can bring about the correct result through a specific constructed method. 

Furthermore, in this specific case, the calculation for the **Count of Days** is not required because the positions of the Sun and Moon are derived from the **remainder of the omitted lunar days** <term>avama-śeṣa</term> <note>Omitted lunar days, or "lost days," occur because a lunar day (tithi) is shorter than a solar day.</note>. This is achieved through the conjunction <note>original: "ca-kāra"; the grammatical use of "and" to link concepts</note> of various factors. In the matter of calculating the collection of days for the northern years <note>referring to the Uttarāyaṇa or northern transit</note>, one asks: "Which method of construction is used?" This means that the information has been "given" <term>datta</term> or assigned. This indicates that the calculation is performed through a **mathematical riddle** <term>kuhaka</term> <note>original: "kuhakena"; 'Kuhaka' refers to complex algebraic puzzles, often involving indeterminate equations, used to test an astronomer's skill.</note>. || 3 ||

He now explains a special rule regarding the **remainder of the intercalary months** <term>adhimāsa-śeṣa</term>. If one wishes to find the day of the week for a desired time, and if the **Count of Days** is reduced by one in relation to the intercalary month remainder... From the intercalary month, in sequence, a previous success is not achieved. When the **Count of Days** is intended by the teacher to be the cause, and if it is reduced by one, then the calculation of the **Count of Days** follows the remainder of the intercalary months in order. The previously mentioned month is not joined; instead, it remains at the end. For a desired measurement, one must apply an additive <term>kṣepa</term> <note>A constant value added to an equation to balance it or account for an epoch's starting position.</note>. This is not previously established but is merely the subject of the question.

If, based on the assumption of the **intercalary remainder** <term>adhi-śeṣa</term>, a question is heard where it is intended by the questioner that the terms should be **reduced** <term>apavartita</term> <note>Simplified to their lowest common denominator.</note>, and if there is a remainder-less state involving the number twenty-four, then the answer is not suitable for giving a result. In the occasion of explaining the rationale, it is necessary to **reduce** <term>apavartana</term> both the dividend and the divisor. If the remainder is greater than the result, it must certainly be reduced. 

If the reduction is not performed as described in the upcoming method, and one tries to divide using a divisor that has not been reduced and a dividend that has not been reduced, then a failure of the mathematical operation occurs. A remainder that has not been reduced is simply an accumulation of the **reducer** <term>apavartaka</term> <note>The common factor used to simplify a fraction.</note>; therefore, whatever reduction is possible must be performed. Thus, the simplified remainder is merely an accumulation of the values. It is for this reason that the calculated intercalary remainder is brought to completion.

# Calculations for the Intercalary Months and Day-Counts

The future intercalary months <term>natādhimāsa</term> <note>Sanskrit: *natādhimāsa*; these are the extra months yet to come in a specific cosmic cycle.</note> and the elapsed intercalary months <term>vigatādhimāsa</term> <note>Sanskrit: *vigatādhimāsa*; the extra months that have already passed since the start of the epoch.</note> are determined by the values [encoded in the verse as] 8, 9, 3, 5 and the zeros, skies, and fire <note>The text uses *bhūta-saṃkhyā*, a system where words represent numbers (e.g., "fire" = 3, "sky" = 0). These represent the large divisors used in medieval Indian astronomy.</note>. 8... regarding the future and past intercalary months.

The measure of the **multiplier** <term>koṭṭāha</term> <note>Sanskrit: *koṭṭāha* or *kuttaka*; a mathematical method, often called "the pulverizer," used to solve indeterminate equations in astronomy.</note> for the days is determined in sequence. By the power of seventy-seven, the movement of the **elapsed lunar** <term>gata-cāndra</term> <note>Referring to the time elapsed in the lunar calendar.</note> cycle is established here.

Furthermore, regarding the remainder of the intercalary month: it is multiplied by eleven minus seven [four], and another is multiplied by nine, ten, and the elements [twenty-four]. In the **first position** <term>prathama-sthāne</term>, the digits are 66388 <unclear>remainder</unclear>. In the **second position** <term>dvitiya-sthāne</term>, the digits are 548 <unclear>...</unclear> <unclear>remainder</unclear>. 

Sequentially, in the first position, are the **elapsed intercalary months** <term>gatādhimāsa</term>. In the second part, there are the future intercalary months, and the **calculated days** <term>koṭṭa-dināni</term> <note>Calculated days derived from the Kuttaka method.</note> which are subtracted from the total days. This pair is placed in multiple positions by means of the multiplier of seventy-seven. The **intercalary months** <term>adhimāsa</term> begin from this; by adding seventy-seven, the elapsed intercalary months are found. By adding seventy-seven to the second, the second is found, and so on, repeatedly and in many ways.

Thus, the elapsed intercalary months and the corresponding calculated day-counts are resolved through a single **multiplier** <term>koṭṭāha</term>. This is done in various ways to establish the positions of the **Sun and the Moon** <note>Sanskrit: *sūrya-candrayoḥ*.</note>. Now, the result of the desired calculation is stated. Thus, the **remainder of omitted days** <term>avama-śeṣa</term> <note>Sanskrit: *avama-śeṣa*; the remainder left over when calculating "omitted" or lunar days that are shorter than solar days.</note> mentioned before, being multiplied by the "skies" and "elements" [large numerical factors] and then divided, gives the two-fold result.

<margin>Nitya-vi[noda] <note>original: "नित्यवि·" (Nityavi); likely the title of the work, *Nitya-vinoda*.</note>

In what I have composed, where the **remainder of omitted lunar days** <term>avama-śeṣa</term> <note>Sanskrit: *avama-śeṣa*; the fractional remainder left over when calculating "lost" lunar days (tithis) to synchronize the lunar calendar with solar days.</note> is **divided by four** <term>caturbhakta</term>, in that context, for the subsequent purpose <unclear>avitivyaṣṭa</unclear> omitted-remainder.

If the assumed omitted-remainder is divided by four and it is not "without remainder" (meaning it leaves a fraction), then in the solar calculation, such a state would not have existed twelve **day-counts** <term>ahargaṇa</term> <note>Sanskrit: *ahargaṇa*; the total number of elapsed days from a fixed epoch used as the basis for all planetary calculations.</note> prior to that omitted-remainder. 

**The Proof and Rationale** <term>upapatti</term>:
In this matter, regarding the days of the Earth-rotation and the measure of the **Age** <term>yuga</term> <note>An immense cycle of time in Indian cosmology.</note>, why is this done? By taking the day-count, the omitted-remainder is calculated based on the total omitted days of the Yuga. That subsequent omitted-remainder is related to the **lunar days of the Yuga** <term>yuga-cāndra-dina</term>. The omitted days of the Yuga are not separate; the lunar days of the Yuga take the form of the **divisor** <term>hāra</term>. In the process of calculation, the other remainder is produced, and the divisor is established as the lunar days of the Yuga.

In the proof that will be explained further, one must necessarily perform the **reduction** <term>apavartanā</term> <note>Sanskrit: *apavartanā*; the mathematical process of simplifying a fraction or ratio by dividing by a common factor.</note> of the **divisor** <term>bhājaka</term> and then proceed. According to the method to be described, the remainder should be reduced by four; otherwise, a total error would result in the calculation. For this reason, if the assumed omitted-remainder divided by four is not without remainder, it is considered incorrect. And this <margin>Rāma <note>Possibly a reference to the astronomer Rāma-daivajña or a specific commentary.</note></margin> is useful for the calculation. The omitted-remainder... has been described by the practitioners. The lunar days of the Yuga [should be divided] by four...

<meta>This page continues the technical astronomical calculations from page 50. While the previous page discussed the remainder of omitted lunar days (avama-śeṣa), this page focuses on the computation of the elapsed lunar day-count (Ahargana) and the specific mathematical rationale (upapatti) using the "Rule of Three."</meta>

The elapsed **lunar day-count** <term>cāndra-dina-gaṇa</term> <note>Sanskrit: *gata-cāndra-dina-gaṇa*; the total number of lunar days that have passed since the start of a specific epoch.</note> is the projection beginning from the year; the lunar days elapsed in the world are many. In the 16th [step] or second section:

Starting from that, for the desired time, the elapsed [days] are multiplied and divided by the prescribed methods. By this method, the elapsed days and the day of the week are determined. Here, the previous 17 [step] involves the solar days produced by the elapsed lunar days. When these are divided by seven, the remainder indicates the day of the week. By these calculations, the result of the lunar day-count is obtained.

The **day-count** <term>ahargaṇa</term> <note>Sanskrit: *ahargaṇa*; literally 'heap of days,' the standard unit of time in Indian astronomy for calculating planetary positions.</note> is multiplied by the intercalary months and divided by the lunar days of the Age. By this method, the days are determined. Now, the elapsed lunar days are converted into the portion of the **Sun** <term>sūrya</term>. This means they are calculated through the elapsed **lunar days** <term>tithi</term> <note>Sanskrit: *tithi*; a lunar day, roughly 1/30th of a lunar month.</note> starting from the months.

When multiplied by twelve and divided, the result for the Sun and **Moon** <term>candra</term> is obtained in the calculation. The result is "Rama" <note>In the Katapayadi or Bhutasankhya system, "Rama" signifies the number 3.</note>. By the aforementioned methods, the result for the 69th [step] in the second question is determined.

The Sun and Moon are equal here at 18. In the answer to the question regarding the remainder 69, he clarifies the first answer with two verses. Here is the **mathematical proof** <term>upapatti</term> <note>Sanskrit: *upapatti*; the logical rationale or demonstration of why a mathematical formula works.</note>: The constant is 16. Using these two, the **Rule of Three** <term>trairāśi</term> <note>Sanskrit: *trairāśi*; a mathematical proportion where a fourth unknown is found from three known quantities (e.g., if A yields B, what does C yield?).</note> is applied.

The **dividend** <term>bhājya</term>, the **divisor** <term>hāra</term>, and the **additive** <term>kṣepa</term> <note>These represent the variables in the equation: (Multiplier × Dividend + Additive) / Divisor = Quotient.</note> must be reduced by a common factor <term>apavartana</term> <note>Sanskrit: *apavartana*; the process of simplifying a fraction or equation by dividing by the Greatest Common Divisor (GCD).</note> to make the Pulverizer possible. If the dividend and divisor are divisible by a certain number, but the additive is not divisible by that same number, then the calculation is invalid. This is because a solution is impossible, as stated in the work **Yoga Shiromani** <note>Likely referring to a specific section of Bhaskara's *Siddhānta Śiromaṇi* or a related astronomical manual.</note>.

...In such cases involving the Pulverizer, what is the specific rule regarding the multipliers? For the sake of clarity, first, the reduction must be performed by a common whole number; otherwise, the Pulverizer calculation cannot be successfully completed.

It is established that if the dividend and divisor are reduced by some factor, but the additive is not reduced by that same factor, then the "purification" of the equation is faulty. This implies that the dividend, multiplied by any multiplier and added to the additive, would not result in a zero remainder when divided by the divisor. || 21 ||

<meta>This page continues the mathematical exposition of the "Pulverizer" (Kuttaka) method, transitioning from the conceptual justification on the previous page to the specific algebraic steps for simplifying equations. It focuses on finding the Greatest Common Divisor (GCD) to reduce the terms before solving the indeterminate equation.</meta>

<margin>Rama</margin> <note>original: "रामः"; likely a shortened invocation to the deity Rama or a scribal mark.</note>
...and <unclear>r</unclear> v <unclear>9</unclear> <note>original: "९"; the number 9 or a similar notation</note> when divided mutually, the remainder that is found is the **common factor** <term>apavartana</term> <note>Sanskrit: *apavartana*; the Greatest Common Divisor (GCD) used to simplify ratios.</note>. By that common factor, the **dividend** <term>bhājya</term>, the **divisor** <term>hāra</term>, and the **additive** <term>kṣepa</term> are divided; they then become known as **firm** <term>dṛḍha</term> <note>Sanskrit: *dṛḍha*; meaning "firm" or "solid," referring to numbers that are reduced to their simplest form or are relatively prime.</note>. By these two methods of mutual division, the remainder which occurs is the common factor for both. By that factor, they are both divided without remainder. Thus it is said: when the divisor and dividend are divided, the additive must also be **divisible** <term>bhājanīya</term> by that same factor. By that remainder, and again by the remainder of the divisor, one should continue the mutual division again and again.

If the remainder should be **unity** <term>rūpa</term> <note>original: "रूपं"; literally "form" or "shape," but in Sanskrit mathematics, it specifically denotes the integer 1.</term>, then they have no common factor. From that point, the remainder of the divisor should divide the dividend. This process of simplification is for the dividend and divisor; the last remainder is called the common factor. Thus, everything—including the additive—must be divisible by it. When the dividend, divisor, and additive have been divided by this common factor, they are "firm." These "firm" dividends, divisors, and additives are used for the **Pulverizer** <term>kuṭṭaka</term> calculation that follows. The author now repeats the method with two verses, beginning with verse 22:

> Mutually divide the firm dividend and divisor until the remainder in this process becomes **unity** <term>rūpa</term>. Place the **quotients** <term>phala</term> <note>Sanskrit: *phala*; literally "fruit," meaning the result or quotient of a division.</note> one below the other in a column. Below them, the additive is to be placed, and finally, a zero is placed at the end. || 23 ||

By multiplying the term above by the one below, the remainder is found...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the "Pulverizer" (Kuttaka) method. Having established the Greatest Common Divisor (GCD) to reduce the dividend and divisor to their "firm" (relatively prime) states, the text now describes the specific algorithmic steps for arranging the resulting quotients and calculating the solution.</meta>

One should divide repeatedly. In this pair of quantities, let the upper be divided by the **reduced** <term>dṛḍha</term> <note>referring to the numbers after they have been divided by their GCD</note> divisor. The result is the quotient; the other is the multiplier. The previously mentioned **reduced dividend** <term>dṛḍhabhājya</term> and **divisor** <term>hāra</term> are divided mutually, one by the other, as long as the procedure requires, until the **remainder** <term>śeṣa</term> is **one** <term>rūpa</term> <note>original: "एक संख्या" / "रूप"; the goal of the Euclidean algorithm step is to reach a remainder of 1</note>. If a remainder of one is not reached, then there is no **increase** <term>kṣapti</term> <note>meaning no integer solution exists for the given additive</note>. 

The quotients obtained in this sequence should be placed one below the other. These quotients are arranged in a **pair-wise chain** <term>valli</term> <note>literally "vine"; in Indian mathematics, this refers to the vertical column or "vine-like" structure used to resolve continued fractions or the Kuttaka algorithm</note>. If only one quotient is obtained, place one; if two are obtained, then two should be placed; if many are obtained, then many should be placed. 

At the bottom of this **vine** <term>valli</term> of quotients, the **additive** <term>kṣepa</term> <note>the constant term being added in the linear equation</note> must be placed. This means the additive is situated at the very end, below the quotients. Furthermore, a **zero** <term>śūnya</term> is placed at the very bottom. 

Then, the process of calculation moves upward: 
Multiply the term above by the one below it, and add the result to the term situated below that. This process of multiplying and adding <note>this refers to the process of "collapsing" the vine to find the values of the variables</note> should be performed repeatedly. In this pair of quantities produced, the upper quantity is divided by the **reduced dividend** <term>dṛḍhabhājya</term>, and the remainder is the desired result. The other quantity is divided by the **reduced divisor** <term>dṛḍhahāra</term>, and the remainder is the multiplier. This calculation is performed according to the established method.

**Reduced** <term>kṛśīkṛta</term> means "made thin," or rather, "evaporated" <note>original: "अवशोषित" (avaśoṣita); here used metaphorically to describe reducing a large number to its simplest form by dividing out common factors</note>. This is what is meant by the "evaporated quantity." Because of that multiplier, the **dividend** <term>bhājya</term> is multiplied—for example, by 68—and then divided by the divisor. From the **remainder** <term>śeṣa</term> of that division, the desired **multiplier** <term>guṇa</term> and **dividend-quantity** <term>vibhājya-rāśi</term> are found. This is how the **special result** <term>viśeṣa</term> 24 is known. 

Now, the text speaks regarding the specific arrangement for the **odd** <term>viṣama</term> status:

In this way, when the calculation of the chain is completed, if the quotients are **odd** <term>viṣama</term> in number <note>meaning there is an odd number of terms in the vertical column of quotients</note>, then the **quotient** <term>labdhi</term> and **multiplier** <term>guṇa</term> obtained from the process must be **subtracted** <term>viśodhya</term> from their respective **reduction-divisors** <term>takṣaṇa</term>. If they are not subtracted, the results will not be correct. This is the accepted method. 

When the mutual division <note>the Euclidean algorithm step</note> is finished, if the quotients are **even** <term>sama</term>—one or two or more—then that result is used as is. But if the quotients are **odd** <term>viṣama</term> <note>original: "विषम"; specifically referring to the parity of the number of quotients in the chain</note>, the rule of subtraction applies. For example, if there is only one quotient, it is an odd case. The **quotient and multiplier** <term>labdhi-guṇa</term> obtained through the process are subtracted from the **reduction-divisor** <term>takṣaṇa</term> <note>original: "तक्षण"; the value used to reduce the result to its smallest positive form</note>. 

The term **reduction** <term>takṣaṇa</term> refers to the **time** <margin>M:</margin> or the limit <note>original: "तक्षणः"; derived from the root meaning to pare down or plane away</note>. Therefore, they are subtracted from their own respective "reduction-divisors"—specifically, from the **divisor** <term>hara</term> 68. The results are the true **dividend-quantity** <term>vibhājya</term> and multiplier.

Above, for the **dividend** <term>vibhājya</term>, the remainder is taken from the reduction. Now, the text speaks of the method for attaining the multiplier and the special case of the remainder:

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical explanation of the Pulverizer (Kuttaka) method, specifically the rules for adjusting the multiplier and quotient based on the "even" or "odd" count of the calculation chain and the nature of the additive or subtractive constant.</meta>

### The Rule of Even and Odd Results

When the number of quotients in the chain is **even** <term>sama</term>, the **multipliers** <term>guṇa</term> that are reached are correct as they are. This is the result produced from a process of **addition** <term>yoga</term> <note>original: "योग ज" (yoga-ja); refers to equations of the form ax + c = by where the constant 'c' is added</note>. 

However, in the case of a result born from **subtraction** <term>viyoga</term> <note>original: "वि योग जे" (viyoga-je); refers to equations where the constant is subtracted, ax - c = by</note>, the multipliers are those obtained by subtracting the preliminary results from their respective divisors. 

If the chain is reached through an **even** count, the results are used directly. If they are linked to the **addition** process, then the fruit <term>phala</term> or result is positive. But if the calculation involves **subtraction**, then the final results must be adjusted to account for the difference. One must understand the distinction between the **positive** <term>dhana</term> and **negative** <term>ṛṇa</term> states in this attainment. 

When the quotients are **even**, the **multipliers** <term>guṇa</term> are satisfied through the addition-based method. But when the quotients are **odd**, the results must be subtracted from the **divisors** <term>takṣaṇa</term> to find the true values. This ensures the **success** <term>siddhi</term> of the calculation. In all cases involving the **Pulverizer** <term>yoga-ja kuttaka</term>, the multipliers are reduced when the count is odd.

Through this logic, the specific nature of the **positive wealth** <term>dhana</term> and the **subtraction** is managed. If the multiplier and quotient are obtained through the even chain, they are the "pleasant" or direct results; if through the odd chain, they are "reduced" or subtracted results.

This concludes the demonstration of the **special result** <term>viśeṣa</term> 26.

### The Fixed Pulverizer

Next, we consider the rule for the **Fixed Pulverizer** <term>sthira kuta</term> <note>Also known as the "Constant Pulverizer," this refers to a generalized solution where the additive/subtractive constant is 1, allowing any other constant to be solved by simple multiplication</note>, beginning with the initial steps of the procedure...

<summary>This page defines the final adjustments for the Kuttaka (Pulverizer) method, explaining how to modify the multiplier and quotient based on whether the number of quotients in the calculation chain is even or odd, and whether the equation uses addition or subtraction. It concludes the 26th specific case and introduces the "Fixed Pulverizer" technique.</summary>

<margin>Commentary on the *Mitaksara* <note>original: "Mitāvi"; likely an abbreviation for Mitākṣarā-vivaraṇa, a known commentary</note></margin>

Regarding the phrase "for each of the remainders," there would be a great burden <note>original: "gauravāt"; refers to the mathematical complexity or tediousness of calculating every remainder individually</note> in the derivation. Therefore, one should assume a specific form for the values to be solved. When the multipliers and the reduced <term>apavartita</term> <note>refers to numbers reduced by a common factor to simplify the equation</note> values are to be found, the remainder is determined through this process. 

According to the method stated by the master **Bhaskara** <note>Bhaskara II, the 12th-century mathematician and author of the *Lilavati*</note>, the remainder of the elapsed days <term>ahargaṇa</term> <note>the total number of days passed since the beginning of a specific epoch</note> is found. This method is consistent with the *Sisyadhivriddhida* <note>original: "śiṣyadhī"; refers to the astronomical treatise by Lalla meant to "increase the intellect of students"</note>, where the intercalary remainder <term>adhiśeṣa</term> is treated as the additive constant in the calculation. 

By following the aforementioned method of the **Pulverizer** <term>kuṭṭaka</term> <note>a method for solving indeterminate equations of the form ax + c = by</note>, if one tries to find the multiplier and the result for every individual remainder, it would lead to excessive labor. For this reason, we assume the intercalary remainder <term>adhiśeṣa</term> to be the additive constant, and then the multiplier is solved. 

<margin>Rama <note>likely the name of the scribe or a secondary commentator</note></margin>

When a desired intercalary remainder is chosen, the multiplier and the result are obtained. These results, when combined with their respective divisors, can produce many different multipliers and results. This is the logic presented in the works of **Bhaskara**. Following this path leads to many variations in the calculation of the **Pulverizer** <term>kuṭṭaka</term>.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the calculation of the Sum of Elapsed Days (Ahargana). It moves from general principles of the Pulverizer method to specific applications found in the works of Bhaskara II, particularly concerning the remainders of intercalary months and omitted lunar days.</meta>

The elapsed intercalary days <term>gatādhimāsa-dina</term> <note>the number of days belonging to "extra" months added to the lunar calendar to keep it synchronized with the solar year</note> are calculated from the total count of days. In this process, even if the multiplier for the elapsed days exceeds three hundred by one, there is no practical purpose in pursuing a result that leads to "ill-repute" <note>original: "apakiirti-phala"; likely a metaphorical way of describing a mathematical error or a method that deviates from established astronomical tradition</note>. Instead, the calculation should begin from the remainders starting with the first day of the bright fortnight of the month of **Chaitra** <note>the first month of the Hindu lunar calendar</note> and proceed until the current day.

The sum of elapsed days <term>ahargaṇa</term> is determined by rejecting the errors in the calculation of the lunar months. Efforts are made to ensure the accuracy of this day-count. Specifically, the remainder of the omitted lunar days <term>avama-dina-śeṣa</term> <note>omitted days are "lost" days in the lunar calendar where a lunar date (tithi) ends before it can be assigned to a sunrise</note> must be found. From that, one considers the intercalary remainder <term>adhiśeṣa</term> and the omitted-day remainder in relation to the number of lunar days passed since the beginning of **Chaitra**.

This is the meaning of the method of expansion stated by the master **Bhaskara** <note>Bhaskara II, the renowned 12th-century mathematician</note> in the *Chapter on Mean Motion* <term>Madhyamādhikāra</term> of his great work, the *Crest-Jewel of Siddhantas* <note>original: "Siddhānta Śiromaṇi"</note>. 

The rule there states: 
When the remainders are divided... for the Sun and the Moon... [the calculation involves] a period of three hours <note>original: "trimuhūrta"</note>. The sum of days ending at the transition starting from **Chaitra** is thus clarified. The positions of the Sun and Moon are determined precisely by these remainders. When these are subtracted from a hundred thousand and adjusted by their own positive values, the correct method is established.

In this matter, a wise person, free from personal bias, should perform the operations according to the rules of their own school of thought. This method regarding the remainders is applied to the two "Luminous Ones" <note>original: "puṣpavantau"; a poetic term for the Sun and Moon</note>. The intent is that the calculation of the Sun and Moon's positions is completed through this approach.

When the remainder of the revolutions is multiplied by twelve and divided by the desired civil days, the result is the **<term>Rashi</term>** <note>the zodiac signs</note>. 

When the [remaining] remainder is multiplied by thirty and divided by the divisor, the result is the **<term>Bhaga</term>** <note>degrees</note>. 

When the [next] remainder is multiplied by sixty and divided by the divisor, the result is the **<term>Kala</term>** <note>minutes of arc</note>. 

When the [final] remainder is multiplied by sixty and divided by the divisor, the result is the **<term>Vikala</term>** <note>seconds of arc</note>. 

The leftover is known as the "remainder of the seconds." For the purpose of obtaining the result consisting of the collection of signs and seconds, the remainder of the revolutions is multiplied by twelve...

That which is equal to the remainder of the clear ascending nodes <note>original: "spaṣṭātraroha-śeṣa"; refers to the mathematical remainder in calculating the position of the lunar nodes or planetary intersections</note>, even when discarded, is not specified among the nine planets. Regarding the knowledge of the **<term>Ahargaṇa</term>** <note>original: "ahargaṇa"; literally "the heap of days," referring to the total count of civil days elapsed since the beginning of a specific astronomical epoch</note> derived from the remainder of the revolutions, he <note>the author or commentator</note> repeats the words of **<term>Bhāskara</term>** <note>Bhāskara II, the 12th-century astronomer. <margin>Rāma</margin> <note>Likely referring to Rāma Daivajña, a prominent 16th-century commentator on Bhāskara's works</note>
"The revolutions, when multiplied by the multiplier and divided by the [divisor], should be purified <note>original: "śuddhāḥ"; in this mathematical context, it means the division results in a whole number with zero remainder</note>." The **Ahargaṇa** multiplied by the number of revolutions and divided by the **Kudina** yields the elapsed revolutions as the quotient; the remainder is the **<term>Bhagaṇa-śeṣa</term>** <note>the fractional part of a planet's current revolution, expressed as a remainder</note>. Therefore, by which the **Ahargaṇa** and the revolution remainders are divided by the **Kudina**, they become purified and leave no remainder <note>original: "niḥśeṣaḥ"</note>. Because the specific name of the revolution is not required at the conclusion, the **Ahargaṇa** can be determined in many ways through the repetition of the **Kudina** as an additive <note>original: "kṣepaka"; a constant added in a linear indeterminate equation of the form ax + c = by</note>.

Multiplied by seven, 53 <note>original: "saptāmāhata"; potentially referring to a multiplier of 7 used in the calculation</note>, divided by the arms of the earth <note>original: "kodvāhu-bhakta"; a poetic numerical expression likely referring to the number 2 or a specific astronomical constant</note>, and reduced by the past <term>intercalary months <note>original: "adhimāsa"; extra lunar months added to the calendar to align the lunar year with the solar year</note></term> that have been multiplied by thirty <note>original: "khāgni-hata"; literally 'multiplied by sky (0) and fire (3)', representing the number 30</note>.

In this context, the elapsed time beginning with the <term>intercalary months <note>original: "antarādya"</note></term> is calculated in <term>inverse order <note>original: "viloma"; the process of working backward from a result to the initial parameters</note></term>. In the achievement of naming the years, months, and lunar days from the day-count, there is an inverse derivation. The calculation of the lords of the days is easy; in the derivation of the sum created by the day-count, there is an inverse derivation. This is the meaning of the statement regarding the "well-spoken words" <note>original: "sagame-sūkta"</note>.

Furthermore, when the occurrence of the day-count falls here, those [values] are brought forward by the years. In those [calculations], the day-count, the count of signs <note>original: "abhra"; literally 'cloud', but also a numerical symbol for zero or a large set</note>, and the group of lunar days are established. At midnight, when there is an occurrence of eleven, the lunar days are not [calculated] by the days starting from the "fire-less" intercalary months <note>original: "niragnādhimāsa"; a technical term possibly referring to a specific state of the intercalary month calculation</note>.

### The Necessity of Mathematical Operations

By means of <term>mathematical operations <note>original: "gaṇitakriyā"</note></term>, one calculates the inverse motion <note>likely referring to retrograde motion or calculating backwards from a known position</note>. This is done according to the additions prescribed in the **Siddhānta Shiromani** <note>The "Crown of Treatises," a foundational 12th-century work by Bhāskara II</note>. Without these operations, the true motion of the <term>mean planets <note>original: "madhyamagraha"; the position of a planet calculated as if it moved at a constant speed in a perfect circle</note></term> cannot be understood. A calculator who is not skilled in these inverse operations cannot succeed, for without them, the intended results are impossible to achieve. 

In the **Siddhānta Shiromani**, it is stated that the method for deriving the <term>true planet <note>original: "sphuṭagraha"; the actual observed position of a planet, accounted for its elliptical orbit and varying speeds</note></term> from the mean planet is the most excellent. To reach the true position, one must first proceed from the mean position. This is why it is taught in this manner. Without this specific method, the calculation is impossible. Therefore, to resolve any confusion or disagreement, this universal explanation is provided. Because such a guide is rare to find in one’s hand, I bow to him as I would to a divine treasure. [Verse 57]

This process of <term>iterative refinement <note>original: "saṃskāra"; a corrective process or calculation applied to astronomical data</note></term> begins with the calculation for Mars and the other planets, ensuring the true positions are firmly established...

### The Equation of Center for the Sun and Moon

The clarity of the **correction for the equation of center** <note>original: "mandaphala-saṃskāra"</note> has been stated. Just as for **Mars and the other planets** <note>original: "bhaumādīnām"</note>, where the logic of the equation of center correction is joined with the **equation of conjunction** <note>original: "śīghraphala"; a second major correction required for the five planets but not for the Sun or Moon</note>. Through **observation** <note>original: "vedha"; refers to verifying mathematical models through visual astronomical observation</note>, the proof of the equation of center is established as either an addition or a subtraction.

This specific mathematical operation is considered primary. By using the result of the equation of center, the clarity of the planetary positions is achieved. This process refutes any false conclusions regarding their positions. For those experts seeking certainty, the Sun and Moon are repeatedly corrected by this equation. When this is done, the two luminaries are considered **"More True"** <note>original: "spaṣṭatarī"; having a higher degree of accuracy in their observed longitudinal position</note>.

This means that the **True Sun** and **True Moon** <note>original: "spaṣṭau sūrya candrāv"</note> are derived with logical proof. By using the nature of the **anomaly** <note>original: "kendra"; the angular distance of a planet from its apogee</note>, the equation of center is calculated once.

The rule for application is as follows:
- In the anomaly beginning with **Aries** <note>original: "meṣādi"</note>, the result is **added**.
- In the anomaly beginning with **Libra** <note>original: "tulādi"</note>, the result is **subtracted**.

Thus, the **Mean Sun and Moon** <note>original: "madhyamau sūrya candrāu"</note> are corrected. This is entirely appropriate, as these positions are produced within the **eccentric circle** <note>original: "pratimaṇḍala"; a geometric model where the planet moves on a circle whose center is offset from the Earth</note>. Through the correction for the equation of center, the clarity of the final result is established, starting from the mean position as it exists.

# Siddhāntatattvaviveka

Regarding what was previously stated, in their opinion, the operation for the two <note>referring to the Sun and Moon</note> is done once. In this view, those two forms and their duality are considered to have a single form. Through the definition of the resulting value, the **Equation of Center** <note>original: "mandaphala"</note> is calculated. For this reason, in the inverse seventh <note>referring to the seventh sign or a specific mathematical house</note>, the values starting from eleven are added. From those, having been joined with the Equation of Center, the **True position** <note>original: "spashta"</note> is obtained. 

According to the method mentioned, by that Equation of Center, those two **True Anomalies** <note>original: "sarpa-kendrau"; "sarpa" usually refers to the nodes, but here likely a transcription error for "spashta" (True)</note> are determined. Because of this, the rejection of the **True Anomaly** corrected by the Equation of Center is addressed. He shows those two corrected positions. In another way, according to the stated method, those two anomalies corrected by the Equation of Center are not the **Mean** <note>original: "madhyama"</note> forms. 

It has been said that the True state arises from that which is corrected by the Equation of Center calculated from the Mean position. In that True state, the Mean position becomes corrected by the Equation of Center calculated from the Mean. In that context, because of the absence of a Mean position derived from a True calculation, the position corrected by the Equation of Center is not [inherently] True. Therefore, its nature as such is somewhat refined. Thus, the corrected result is the **True Mean** <note>an intermediate step in Indian astronomy where a position is more accurate than Mean but not yet fully True</note>. By removing that, and through the addition of the correction, the Equation of Center calculated [only] from the Mean is established. (7)

<page-num>65</page-num>

# Siddhāntatattvaviveka

When there is a contradiction between the two opinions, it is said that the seventh <note>likely referring to a specific mathematical step or house in the calculation</note> is superior. Likewise, starting from the **Mean** <term>madhyama; the average position of a planet ignoring irregularities</term>, the addition or subtraction <note>original: "dhana-ṛṇa"</note> is stated. This is my opinion; this is the view I favor. Why is that? To this, they say that the resulting calculation is logical. This means that according to the previous school of thought, the results were produced differently. In that previous school, because those results were obtained through an iterative process <term>asakṛt; a repetitive calculation used to reach a limit</term>, and because the addition or subtraction lacked a clear logical derivation <note>original: "niryukti"</note>, it is considered incorrect.

Now, if in this school—even in the absence of a particular form—the established **Siddhāntas** <term>siddhānta; a formal scientific treatise or established system</term> are followed, then the process should be like that of Mars and the other planets <note>original: "bhaumādi"</note>. Because of the instruction to provide the sequence of results, which is also stated in the established systems, the true nature of the calculation is preserved. By following that logic, the status of the **Sun and Moon** <note>original: "sarpa-candrayoḥ"; though "sarpa" means serpent, it is likely an OCR error for "sūrya" (Sun) or a reference to the Nodes</note> being calculated iteratively is established by tradition.

Is this contrary to the teachings of the Sages <term>ārṣa; originating from the divine seers</term>? To this, he says that it is explicitly defined in the **Siddhāntas** of the Sun, the Moon, and others. This means that this method has been stated in the established treatises.

<margin>Rāma</margin> <note>This likely refers to Rāma Daivajña or another commentator whose specific opinion is being noted here</note>

By saying "the calculation is excellent," the harmony of the system is maintained. Thus, in the **Sūryasiddhānta** <term>The "Sun-Treatise," the most authoritative text of Indian astronomy</term>, it is said that the darkness <note>referring to the Shadow-planet/Node Rāhu</note> is calculated iteratively. There is a statement in the *Ratnasammata* <note>a classic astronomical text</note> that this applies to each one individually. The intention is that there are no obstacles to this logic.

**Demonstration:** <note>original: "upapattiḥ"; a formal proof or derivation in Indian mathematics</note> From one's own planet, when the Slow-Correction <term>mandaphala; the correction for a planet's elliptical orbit</term> is not yet fully applied, and one considers only the **Fast Correction** <term>śīghraphala; the correction for the planet’s relative motion to Earth</term> as the **Slow-Corrected** <term>mandaspaṣṭa; the position after the first major correction</term> position, the Fast Correction is brought forth here. In this way, when the result is applied to one’s own planet, the **Mean Position** <term>madhyama; the average or theoretical position</term> would be obtained; therefore, one should consider the application of the Slow-Correction. 

There, when the sum is applied, it takes the form of the **Mean Position**, known as the **Slow-Corrected** position. By applying this Fast Correction to one’s own planet—or however it may be—the **Precise Mean** <term>sūkṣma-madhyama; the mathematically accurate average position</term> is found. In this context, by performing the **Inverse Operation** <term>vyasta; a reverse calculation used to work backward from an observed position to a theoretical one</term>, the Mean position is indeed obtained.

And on page 69, in the **Chapter on the Three Questions** <term>tri-praśna-adhikāra; the section of astronomical texts dealing with Time, Direction, and Place</term>, he states the subject of the question: *"The numbers six and twenty-two, doubled..."* as stated by **Bhaskara** <note>Bhāskara II, also known as Bhāskarācārya, the author of the Siddhānta Śiromaṇi</note>. In the context of the seven-part division, this is clarified. From this, the wise man understands that from the seven-part calculation, the measure of the corner-shadow for humans occurs in [certain] years. As for the verse *"The numbers six and twenty-two, doubled..."* and so on, regarding the method for clearing the "corner" <note>likely referring to the calculation of a shadow's position relative to the compass points</note> as stated by Bhaskara, it is found in the <margin>Rama <note>An invocation to the deity Rama, common in manuscripts</note></margin> **Crest-Jewel of Systems** <term>Siddhānta Śiromaṇi; the definitive astronomical work of the 12th century</term> in the **Chapter on the Three Questions**. ### Calculation of the Sine of the Corner Gnomon

Having divided by the square of the classes resulting from the **sine of one and a half signs** <term>sārdha-kara-rāśi-ja-jyā; the sine of 45 degrees, as one sign equals 30 degrees</term>, the result of the **sine** <term>jyā</term> and the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> are divided by 65 <note>verse number or constant</note>. From the two gnomons and the calculations of the sines of the shadows and their origins, the power is derived. Thus, the **square of the equinoctial shadow** <term>palabhā-varga; the square of the shadow cast by a 12-digit gnomon at noon on the equinox</term> is doubled, and again increased by **one hundred and forty-four** <note>144 is the square of the standard 12-digit gnomon</note>.

### Calculation of the Square of the Radius and the Gnomon

The **square of the radius** <term>trijyā-varga; the square of the standard 3438' radius used in trigonometry</term> is the true meaning here. From the end of the hemisphere, the **sine of one and a half signs** <term>sārdha-rāśi-ja-jyā; the sine of 45 degrees</term> is multiplied by the square root and divided by five.
