Due to the power of the planetary sphere, regarding the zeniths situated in the directions between the North and South, whether those results of the sphere and the zeniths are in the same or different directions, the **gnomon** <term>śaṅku</term> would be the difference in the **upright side** <term>koṭi; the vertical side of the right-angled triangle formed by the gnomon</term>. Here, for a known gnomon, the base is the shadow of the gnomon... <note>original: "na talā jyākābho"; likely a corrupted transcription of "nara-tala-jyā," referring to the sine of the gnomon's base</note> through the sine of the altitude. From the square roots of the remainders of both sides, after the **extraction of the middle term** <note>original: "madhyāharaṇa"; the historical Indian method for solving quadratic equations, similar to "completing the square"</note>, the measurement is once again known in the calculation.

This follows from a **proportion** <note>original: "anupāta"; the rule of three</note>: If the **radius** <term>trijyā; the standard radius used in trigonometric sines</term> is the hypotenuse and the **sine of the direction** <term>digjyā</term> is the side, then what is the side when the sine of the altitude is the hypotenuse? 

The square of the unknown gnomon is subtracted from the square of the radius. Because the squares of the sides are equal, the mathematical operations are gathered through the process of balancing the equation. This follows the method of **algebra with one unknown** <note>original: "ekavarṇa-bīja"; literally "single-color seed," where 'color' refers to an unknown variable</note> involving the extraction of the middle term. <meta>This page continues the technical mathematical derivation of the equinoctial shadow (palabhā). While the previous page established the general algebraic formula, this page focuses on the specific cases of observations made within the same celestial hemisphere versus different hemispheres, and addresses potential mathematical paradoxes such as division by zero.</meta>

### On the Calculation of the Equinoctial Shadow

When the two "arms" <term>bhujas; the north-south components of the shadow's position</term> are multiplied by the shadow-hypotenuses <term>karṇa</term> of the opposite observations respectively, and they belong to the same direction, the calculation is performed for the same hemisphere. **[Verse] 75.**

In the same way, the **equinoctial shadow** <term>palabhā</term> is obtained through the difference of the results. However, the teacher explains that when the observations occur in different hemispheres <note>original: "vibhinna gole"</note>, the calculation of the equinoctial shadow must be adjusted. To make this meaning clear, in the explanation of this verse, the phrase "in the case of equality" is used. By this method, the rule for this verse is accepted and stated. **[Verse] 76.**

#### Addressing Potential Mathematical Errors

Now, an objection is raised:

-> If the two arms are equal, would the calculation not result in a direct perception of a zero-denominator, or the fallacy of **division by zero**? <note>original: "khaharatva"; in Indian mathematics, dividing by "kha" (zero) was a known problem leading to infinity or undefined results</note> Since the arms are equal, and the shadow-hypotenuses would also be equal, wouldn't this calculation fail everywhere except at the equator? <-

The author responds to this:
In the context of **circle-geometry** <term>vṛttakṣetra</term> and the calculation of these two "arms," the mathematical method described is not flawed. The situation you describe — where the arms are perfectly equal — is not a subject for this specific problem because it would imply the sun is positioned exactly on the **prime vertical** <term>samamaṇḍala; the great circle passing through the east, west, and zenith points</term>.

Therefore, there is no fault in the logic. When the arms are equal, the "subject of the question" <note>the specific astronomical configuration being calculated</note> is absent because there is no difference to measure. Since there is no difference between the two forms, the heat of the "confusion" <note>original: "tāpa"; literally heat or distress, used here metaphorically for intellectual difficulty</note> regarding the equinoctial shadow is removed through this observation.

### Derivation of the Shadow Calculation

The following is the proof for the derivation of the shadow problem <note>original: "dhana-praśna-upapatti"</note>. This means that when the shadow is not clearly perceived, the teacher explains the mathematical form through the two "arms" <term>bhujas; the north-south components of the shadow's position</term>. By using the two arms and the shadow-hypotenuses <term>karṇa</term> at a given time, if there is a difference in their positions, the calculation proceeds in the opposite manner to that previously stated.

When the value of the unknown shadow is sought, it is derived by analyzing the difference between the two hypotenuses. This is done using the method of the "arms" when they are in the same direction. In the **Southern Hemisphere** <term>dakṣiṇa gola</term>, the equinoctial shadow-hypotenuse is added to the arm of the shadow; however, the "arm" itself is considered the equinoctial shadow <term>palabhā</term> in this context. 

<margin>Rama</margin> <note>This is likely an invocation to the deity Rama or the signature of the scribe who copied this portion of the manuscript.</note>

In the **Northern Hemisphere** <term>uttara gola</term>, the "arm" is determined by the difference between the equinoctial shadow and the hypotenuse. In these two methods, the relationship between the hypotenuse and the equinoctial shadow is established. The "arm" is thus the result of the equinoctial shadow's interaction with the specific shadow measured at that moment. These are the variations that occur.

Following this, the rule of proportion <term>anupāta</term> is applied to achieve the desired result. This means that the shadow-hypotenuse being sought is calculated through the proportion of the known values. 

**[Verse] 78.** 

This concludes the brief explanation of the derivation and the method for calculating the shadow when the sun is in the different hemispheres.

### Proof for the Variations of the Shadow

This is to be inferred: the equality of the calculation arises from the six-fold distribution of forms. Through the **Ekarnavi** method <note>original: "ekārṇavī"; likely a reference to a specific oceanic-scale mathematical system or a commentary of that name</note>, the rule of the calculation is easily understood by us.

The entire method is as follows: this involves the "three-eight" <note>the number 24</note> which represents the uniform nature of the forms. This was spoken by **Ramanetra** <note>original: "rāmanetra-uktaḥ"; Ramanetra can be a proper name or a numerical symbol for 23, where Rama = 3 and Netra = 2</note>. 

In that context, regarding the objective of the variations, the experts in this method utilize "one less" <note>the principle of n-1</note> for a single variable. For those who understand the procedure, the method stated is of the nature of the "Giver of Indra's Copper" <note>original: "tāmredradātmakaṃ"; possibly a specific poetic name for a geometric figure or a planetary alignment</note>. Among these, the total count of variations is determined by the **product of numbers starting from one** <term>ekādivarṇāṅkaghātaḥ; the mathematical definition of a factorial, n!</term>. 

The distinctions that manifest there are reached through the **Kapasha** method <note>original: "kapāśo"; a specific algorithmic rule</note>, which extends to six stages. This constitutes the explanation of the **Equinoctial Shadow** <term>palabhā; the shadow cast by a vertical gnomon at noon on the day of the equinox</term>.

This flaw is what we must address. Even when there is a state of **equality** <term>sāmya</term>, because of the difference in the nature of the results produced, the division of the mathematical operation would otherwise lead to an **absurdity** <term>atiprasaṅga; a logical fallacy where a rule is applied so broadly that it becomes meaningless</term>. Therefore, this **equation** <term>samīkaraṇa; the act of making equal or balancing two sides of a calculation</term> is accepted. 

Because of the difference in the inherent nature of the results, the status of the operation would otherwise not be established. For this reason, such an approach contains no error and is the accepted method. ### Page 44

The devotion to this celestial science <note>original: "jyotir"</note> at any desired time, and the understanding of its heat and light, is truly known by **Bhaskara**. For those who are well-versed in the **established astronomical treatises** <term>Siddhānta; traditional Indian astronomical systems</term>, it is understood through the relationship of the **hypotenuse of the shadow** <term>chāyā-karṇa; the distance from the top of the gnomon to the end of its shadow</term> and the **arc-base** <term>cāpa-bhuja; the side of a spherical triangle corresponding to the sine of the arc</term>. The hypotenuse of the shadow is indeed the key. ->Rāma Rāma<- <note>A common scribal invocation or "seal" used to mark the end of a section</note>

In the context of the preservation of time, one asks: for whom and of what kind is the **calculation of the planets** <term>grahagaṇita</term>? From that, the **calculation of the equinoctial shadow** <term>palabhānayana</term> follows. One is not able to cross this "ocean" of calculation without it. This is the difference: indeed, by Bhaskara’s method, from the product of the **declination** <term>krānti; the angular distance of a celestial body north or south of the celestial equator</term> and the shadow-hypotenuse, the **sine** <term>jyā</term> is produced. This is its inherent nature.

From that result, the other **base** <term>bhuja</term> of the arc is established. Through the division of these small units of measurement, the result is combined with its own square. This is stated in the **Siddhānta Shiromani** <note>Bhaskara II's 12th-century masterpiece; the "Crest-jewel of Mathematical Systems"</note>. In that work, a different method is described to remove confusion, as explained by Bhaskara himself in his verses.

The meaning is this: the state of being "divided" arises from the process of **division** <term>haraṇa</term> specifically related to the equinoctial shadow. Likewise, the nature of external division is explained for clarity. Bhaskara’s explanation is intended to support the intelligence of the student and prevent errors. Therefore, in the **Siddhānta Shiromani**, this cause is stated; and in other places also, this intended meaning is refined.

### The Logic of the Proof

The conclusion drawn from the **logical proof** <term>upapatti; a step-by-step demonstration or reasoned justification for a mathematical rule</term> is as follows: even in the case of the **unknown** <term>avyakta; the variable, literally the "unmanifest" quantity</term>, because there is an absence of **positivity** <term>dhanatva; the state of being a positive or additive quantity</term>, there is no path forward. This means that an **inverse root** <term>viparīta mūla; a square root or solution obtained through reversing standard operations, which may result in a negative value</term> is logically unattainable.

One might ask: "Why is the measurement taken in this way?" To this, the author replies: In the common world, people do not lack understanding of standard measurements. However, for the learned, an inversion that is seen to be **non-contradictory** <note>original: "aviruddham"; consistent with the underlying mathematical principles</note> is indeed logically sound. This is the underlying sentiment.

In the third section, regarding the **side** <term>bhujā; literally "arm," referring to a side of a geometric figure or a specific term in a calculation</term> and other elements, the progression is established. When the **equalization** <term>samaśodhana; the fundamental algebraic operation of removing equal quantities from both sides of an equation to isolate the unknown</term> of parts is conducted, there is no inversion. When the equalization of a positive form is established <unclear>as 7</unclear>, a single result is proven in the first position. However, in the second position, due to the nature of the **equalization** <note>original: "śodhana-bhāvāt"; from the state or existence of subtraction/equalization</note>, the values are considered in relation to one another. Thus, the statement regarding **inversion** <term>vaiparītya; the process of reversing operations, such as treating subtraction as addition</term> is made to clarify the nature of the units.

In this context, regarding the equalization of parts, there is a specific discussion. By saying that the measurement is obtained through inversion, one explains the **logic of measurements** <term>māno-papatti; the rational justification for the calculated values</term> in this subject.

### Analysis of the Measurements

One should contemplate the analysis of the measurement <note>original: "mānapanamanaru"; likely referring to the evaluation of the shadow's magnitude</note>. Now, in a situation where the **base shadow** <term>bhujābhā; the shadow cast by the gnomon specifically along the north-south or east-west axis</term> is greater than **twelve** <note>The standard gnomon in Indian astronomy is always 12 angulas (units) high</note>, the same method of **cancellation** <term>sama-śeṣa; the algebraic reduction of terms on both sides of an equation</term> is applied. By means of the **latitude-sine** <term>akṣa-śara; a value derived from the local latitude used in spherical trigonometry</term>, having taken the **square root** <term>varga-mūla</term> of the square of the difference of those [values], it is combined with the **known quantity** <term>vyakta; a constant numerical value</term> to establish the nature of the shadow. 

The existence of the addition or subtraction <note>original: "dhana-ādi"; positive and negative operations</note> in the cancellation process is thus proven. Regarding the **equinoctial shadows** <term>palabhā; the noon shadow at the equinox, used to determine local latitude</term>, it was previously said that by the subtraction of the equinoctial shadow from the shadow's **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa; the diagonal line from the top of the gnomon to the tip of the shadow</term>, the result is established. 

Having consumed <note>used here in the sense of "processed" or "calculated"</note> the tip of the **base** <term>bhuja; the side of the triangle representing the sine</term>, and by unifying the motion of the day, there is no contradiction in the movement. The calculation of the result for the deceased <note>This may be a transcription error for 'vṛtta' (circle/calculation) or a specific astronomical term regarding the 'passed' portion of the day</note> is established in two ways. ### Calculations of the Gnomon and Hypotenuse

Where the essence of the calculation is sought, one takes the square of the **twelve-unit** <note>original: "dvādaśa"</note> **gnomon** <term>śanku; the vertical pillar of a sundial used to measure time and celestial positions</term>. By combining this with the square of the **base shadow** <term>bhujā</term> and taking the square root, the **shadow’s hypotenuse** <term>chāyā-karṇa; the diagonal line connecting the tip of the gnomon to the tip of the shadow</term> is determined. This process should be carried out using the known twelve-unit gnomon as the standard.

These values are then measured against the **equinoctial shadow hypotenuse** <term>palakarṇa; the hypotenuse of the gnomon's shadow at noon during the equinox</term>. In the case of a **southern shadow** <note>original: "dakṣiṇā bhā"</note>, the calculation is determined by the sum of the equinoctial hypotenuse and the **equinoctial shadow** <term>palabhā</term>. The difference arising from the **square of twelve** <note>the constant 144, derived from the 12-unit gnomon</note> is then divided by the specific sign of the zodiac to find the precise measurement.

There are two methods <note>original: "dvi-rīti"</note> for determining the difference in the angles based on the sine of the latitude. From the difference between the resulting **base** <term>bhuja</term> and the calculated "fruit" <note>the result of a mathematical operation</note>, the value of the equinoctial hypotenuse is obtained. If the calculation reaches the intended point, the result is considered verified. According to the various methods found in the **Solar Treatise** <term>Arka-Tantra; an ancient mathematical text focusing on solar positions</term>, these values are adjusted whether they are larger or smaller <margin>Rama</margin> <note>The marginal note "Rama" likely serves as a sacred invocation or indicates the name of the scribe who checked the manuscript</note>.

By following the sequence for the daily rotation of the celestial sphere, the shadow’s hypotenuse is calculated manually. Consequently, the **sine of declination** <term>krāntijyā; the angular distance of the sun north or south of the celestial equator</term>—which is here given the value of **thirteen (13)**—is established based on the hypotenuse of the shadow and the corresponding position.

### The Commentary of Lakshmidatta on the Crest-Jewel of Astronomy

Regarding the calculation performed once or twice, Lakshmidatta—the expert calculator of the *Siddhanta Shiromani* <note>original: "Siddhānta Śiromaṇi Gaṇita Karttā"; the Siddhanta Shiromani is the masterpiece of the 12th-century mathematician Bhaskara II</note>—considers the following: Here, the proof <term>upapatti; a demonstration or rational proof in Indian mathematics</term> for the **sine of declination** <term>krāntijyā; the angular distance of a celestial body from the celestial equator</term>, the hypotenuse, and the product of these values is stated according to the sequence of the half-verse. 

The vessel for calculating entities situated on the sphere <term>gola; refers here to the study of spherical astronomy or the physical celestial globe</term> is described. By utilizing the Rule of Three <term>trairāśika; a mathematical proportion involving three knowns to find an unknown</term> and other methods, they speak of the sine of the altitude <note>original: "jivyā"</note>. This is demonstrated through the tradition of the astronomers. By this means, the clarity of the proof appears to me <note>the commentator</note>. Even through external observation of the Sun <note>original: "arka"</note>, the mathematical work matches the visual appearance. Because the author of the text did not focus on this specific visual proof, the underlying logic must be carefully examined.

### 7. The Definition of a True Astronomer

To this, he provides the answer: When the desired elapsed time in *nadikas* <term>nāḍikā; a unit of time equivalent to 24 minutes</term> is known, its state is determined. 

->“I consider that person to be a true astronomer <term>jyotirvid</term> who understands the calculations regarding his own declination <note>original: "krānta"</note> both mathematically and within the geometry of the sphere.”<-

By this standard, the knowledge of the *nadikas* at a desired time is confirmed by the sine of declination. Those who grasp this are accepted into the ranks of astronomers. I consider such a person to be unparalleled in the study of the sphere.

In the case where the signs are inverted, a **positive value** <term>dhana</term> <note>original: "dhana"; literally "wealth," used in mathematics to denote a positive or additive quantity</note> becomes **negative** <term>ṛṇa</term> <note>original: "ṛṇa"; literally "debt," used to denote a subtractive or negative quantity</note>. Whether the **base-side** <term>bhuja</term> and the sine of declination are increased or decreased, the **series** <term>śreṇi</term> is thus established. 

He mentions the "multiplication by four" <note>This refers to the "rule of four" (caturāha), a method for solving quadratic equations attributed to the mathematician Śrīdhara, where the equation is multiplied by four times the coefficient of the square term to complete the square</note> regarding the **equal squares** <term>varga-sama</term>. On the side where the units are, they are multiplied; then, by the sun-like <note>The number 12 is often symbolized by the "Sun" (Ravi) in technical Sanskrit verse</note> [gnomon] divisor, the units are added and the equal forms are established. 

In the case of the **cosine** <term>koṭi</term>, by the square multiplied by four and the square of half [the middle term]... being the divisor of the double square, it becomes the **middle term** <term>madhyatva</term>. Where the **middle term** <term>madhyama</term> is multiplied by four times the square [coefficient], the **unknown quantity** <term>avyakta</term> <note>original: "avyakta"; literally "the unmanifest," the standard Sanskrit term for an algebraic variable or unknown, like 'x'</note> is placed. When the divisors are multiplied and combined, the square of the **unknown** <term>avyakta-varga</term> becomes equal to the middle. 

This is a great task, but for the sake of obtaining the result of the calculation, one should always use the units. In the place of the **unknown** <term>avyakta</term>, the **middle term** <term>madhyama</term> is projected. In the side of the unknown, the **calculation** <term>karaṇa</term> involves adding or subtracting. The middle term is diminished by its square...

<margin>Rāma</margin>
...for the sake of the **simplification of equals** <term>sama-śodhana</term> <note>original: "sama-śodhana"; the process of clearing an equation by subtracting equal quantities from both sides</note> of the roots. <insert>| Unknown 1, Units 1, Middle 1 |</insert> The **middle term** <term>madhyama</term> is positive or negative in sequence. By the method of **simplification** <term>sama-śodhana-vidhi</term>, having decreased the middle and multiplied... <insert>| Unknown 1, Middle 1 |</insert> Or, what unknown is placed below? Through the equality of the **unknown quantity** <term>avyakta-rāśi</term>, with the value of 65, the divisor is used in the calculation. By the [method of] **Bhāskara** <note>Refers to the 12th-century mathematician Bhāskara II and his work, likely the Bī jaganita (Algebra) or Siddhānta Śiromaṇi</note>, the calculation of the shadow is [determined]...

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical critique of Jñānarāja’s Siddhāntasundara. It explores the algebraic rules for determining when to add or subtract values (positive vs. negative) based on the Sun's position relative to the celestial equator and the length of the gnomon's shadow.</meta>

From the **sine of declination** <term>krāntijyā</term> <note>The R-sine of the Sun's angular distance north or south of the celestial equator</note>, the limit of the **gnomon-value** <term>śaṅkāvadhi</term> <note>The maximum or boundary value for the sine of altitude/gnomon calculation</note> is established. The **mean** <term>madhyama</term> value is characterized as **positive or negative** <term>dhana-ṛṇa</term> based on whether the sine of declination exceeds this gnomon-limit.

When the Sun is associated with the **southern arm** <term>dakṣiṇa-bhuja</term> <note>The southern portion of the meridian arc or the southern direction of the shadow's path</note> and the sine of declination is greater than the gnomon-value, the **derivation** <term>upapatti</term> proves it to be positive. In the algebraic calculation <term>vīja-sthāne</term>, when considering the difference between the squares of these two values, the mean becomes **negative** <term>ṛṇa</term> in the place of the **unknown** <term>avyakta</term> <note>The variable or "unknown" quantity in the equation, equivalent to 'x'</note>.

In the place of the **known** <term>vyakta</term> values, if the sine of declination is the greater value, one takes the difference between the squares of the southern sine of declination and the southern arm. The sine of declination is considered positive within its own arc. This result is then either added to or subtracted from the **square of the mean** <term>madhyama-varga</term>.

Therefore, the nature of the **gnomon** <term>śaṅku</term> <note>A vertical pillar used to measure time and celestial coordinates via its shadow</note> is observed through these forms. Because the lesser is subtracted from the greater, in the **southern hemisphere** <term>dakṣiṇa-gole</term>, as I have stated previously, the mean is negative in the position of the unknown. 

However, in the **northern hemisphere** <term>uttara-gole</term>, as I have previously established, the unknown is **positive** <term>dhana</term> at the sixteenth position <note>referring to a specific step in the calculation or a line in a table</note>, and the mean is subtracted from the unknown. In that first instance, it is treated as a positive value through the process of subtraction.

# Technical Analysis of the Gnomon Calculation

In the second alternative where the **mean** <term>madhyama</term> is subtracted, as mentioned in the root text, the **gnomon** <term>śaṅku</term> <note>A vertical pillar used to measure time and celestial coordinates via its shadow</note> calculation is affected by the magnitude of the **sine of declination** <term>krāntiguṇa</term>. There is a confusion in the proof regarding the "holding of the creeper" <note>likely a reference to a geometric arc or a specific instrumental diagram called a "latā"</note> because no distinction is made for specific locations during **northern declination** <term>uttarāpakarame</term>. In such cases, the sine of declination is clearly greater than the **arm** <term>bāhu</term> <note>The horizontal coordinate of the Sun or its shadow</note>, leading to an inconsistency in the placement of values.

One might object: in the gnomon calculation, the **sum of the half-sides** <term>samabhujārdha-yoga</term> is used with the **square of the mean** <term>madhyama-varga</term>. The logic of the sine of declination being greater or lesser than the gnomon must be applied here. The rule regarding the "addition or subtraction of the mean" <note>original: "madhyona-yug"</note> follows the sequence of the **northern and southern arms** <term>uttara-dakṣiṇa-bhuja</term>. However, this is the reverse of what was stated by **Bhāskara** <note>Bhāskarācārya, the influential 12th-century mathematician and astronomer</note>. If the **root** <term>mūla</term> <note>The result of a square root operation in the equation</note> is greater than the mean in the northern arm, or if it is smaller—

<margin>Rāma <note>Referring to the commentator or a specific authority cited by the scribe</note></margin>

—the arm is smaller than the **sine of declination** <term>krāntijyā</term>. To claim the root is "joined with the mean" in this context is somewhat contradictory. To address this, another fault is shown: even when the root is smaller than the mean in the southern arm, in the **algebraic side** <term>avyakta-pakṣe</term> <note>The portion of the calculation dealing with unknown variables, literally the "unmanifest side"</note>, following the procedures of **Algebra** <term>Bīja-gaṇita</term> (as in the sequence 5, 6...), the mean is subtracted from the root.

It employs algebraic logic to argue how the 'mean' value should be treated when the sine of declination exceeds other coordinates.</summary>

...thus, in the second case, the **equinoctial shadow** <term>palabhā</term> <note>The shadow cast by a vertical gnomon at noon on the day of the equinox</note> is indeed produced by that which is subtracted from the mean. Therefore, in the case of a non-attainment of the **wealth** <term>dhana</term> <note>Here used technically to mean a positive additive value or result</note>, the desired result is stated. For that purpose, he summarizes the forms of the calculation. For that reason, the gnomon is established by the power of its own inclination.

This is the meaning: in the middle of the calculation, even with great intelligence, this was overlooked in the **Algebra** <term>Bījagaṇite</term> <note>A famous 12th-century mathematical treatise by Bhāskara II</note>. By not observing the destruction of the produced value, the fault is not in the logic but in the application.

By this very method, the gift of conciseness is achieved. In this world, that which was stated in the previous verse is now clarified by another verse.

<margin>101</margin>
In these few words, the conflict between the two is resolved. The fault mentioned previously—repeated again and again—is indeed found here. In the case of the **mean value** <term>madhyama</term>, by the process of dividing the result of the distribution, it is combined with the **squares** <term>varga</term> of the values. Multiplied and then combined with the squares of the **declination** <term>krānti</term> and the **gnomon's shadow** <term>chāyā</term>, it is either decreased or increased by the result. This occurs because the **declination-sine** <term>krānti-guṇā</term> is greater than the gnomon itself, and so forth. After the **simplification of the equation** <term>samaśodhana</term> <note>The algebraic process of reducing an equation to its simplest form by removing common terms from both sides</note>, the term for **squaring the unknown** <term>avyakta-vargīkaraṇa-pada</term> <note>In Indian algebra, 'avyakta' refers to the unknown variable, similar to 'x' in modern notation</note> is designated as the 'Hora'. By that, through the mean value and the result of half of its square, and by taking the square root, the possibility of the destruction of the mean is avoided. Thus, the statement "the unknown is the result increased or decreased" is established through logical derivation.

This is clearly explained by the author of the **Siddhānta** <term>Siddhānta-sandarbhakāreṇa</term> <note>Likely referring to Bhāskara II, the author of the Siddhānta Śiromaṇi</note>. Therefore, it should not be misunderstood by the learned. He states the reason: "Because of the previously mentioned..." From the **lengthy method** <term>gurubhūtama-prakāra</term>—
<margin>Rāma</margin>
meaning from that which was said to be increased or decreased by the mean square—this is combined or subtracted. From this, the **concise method** <term>laghubhūtama-prakāra</term> <note>Literally "the very light method," referring to an optimized or more elegant mathematical proof</note> is the cause. There is a "conflict in few words"—meaning a contradiction between the two short statements. Now, one might ask: how can there be a contradiction with the previous statement "increased or decreased by its square"? There should be no doubt regarding the gnomon and the declination here.

<meta>This page continues the technical and philosophical defense of astronomical methodology. # On Logical Derivation and Mathematical Knowledge

Without a **logical derivation** <term>upapatti</term> and vast knowledge, any speech regarding these matters lacks merit. It is through the confusion of these principles that one falls into a state of **foolishness** <term>mūrkhī-bhāva</term>. In response to this, the text says: "He does not truly live." <note>This likely refers to the idea that a scholar without logic is intellectually 'dead' or ineffective.</note>

Now, if it is asked: "Even if one hears the truth, what is the capacity for **imagination** <term>kalpanā-sāmarthya</term> in one who has strayed from the correct path? What will become of them?"

The answer is: **Through logic** <term>tarka</term>. Even if one studies for many years, without reasoning, the truth remains unknown. 

If one further objects: "But is it not possible that the power of human logical derivation could exist in the future, even in a **stone** <term>pāṣāṇa</term>?" 

The answer is: **Like a seed** <term>bīja</term>. <note>This is a classic philosophical analogy: just as a stone cannot sprout because it lacks the 'seed' of potentiality, a mind without the seed of logical reasoning cannot produce knowledge.</note>

Thus, in all cases, where a position is **established** <term>sthāpita</term> through logical proof, it is seen and understood clearly. One who is intent on hiding faults may see them, but they do not truly perceive the reality. 13.

### Regarding the Measurement of Time and Shadows

Next, the text addresses those who are confused:

At the time of measurement, having known the **measure of the shadow** <term>prabhā-māna</term> and the like, one understands the **illumination** <term>prakāśa</term>. <margin>Rāma</margin> <note>original: "Rāmaḥ"; likely a scribal invocation to the deity Rama or a signature.</note>

For the **expert in the sphere** <term>gola-jña</term> <note>An astronomer or cosmographer who understands the celestial sphere.</note>, this knowledge extends to the limits of the universe. For such a person, what is hidden? He understands the **measure of the shadow** <term>prabhā-māna</term> at any given **time** <term>kāla</term>, the resulting **shadow** <term>chāyā</term>, and the **azimuth** <term>digaṃśa</term> <note>The horizontal angle or direction of a celestial object.</note>. This is the light of knowledge found in the books of the masters.

<meta>This page continues the transition from the philosophical defense of astronomical logic to its practical application in gnomonics (the study of shadows) and spherical trigonometry. It connects the "logical seed" mentioned on the previous page to the actual calculation of planetary positions and time.</meta>

One who knows the **celestial sphere** <term>gola: the conceptual model of the heavens as a sphere</term> as if it were a jewel, 33. He exerts effort in the North <note>This likely refers to calculations specific to the northern hemisphere or the northern path of the sun (Uttarayana)</note> and assigns names accordingly.

The measurement of the **sine** <term>jyāguṇā: literally 'the quality of the bowstring,' refers to the trigonometric sine function</term> and the **chord** <term>jīvakā</term> becomes the base arm. From the direction of the observer, the declination is to be known.

From this method of calculation, the conceptualization is established: the **shadow** <term>chāyā</term> is multiplied [by the desired factor] and then **divided by the radius** <term>trijyayā: the standard radius used in Indian trigonometry, often 3438 minutes of arc</term>. This results in the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> of the shadow. The base is the **shadow-tip** <term>chāyāgra</term>, and from the radiation of the light, the signs are determined.

From that known position, the North and South arms are determined respectively. By subtracting the longitude of the sun and other factors, the **sine of declination** <term>krāntijyā: the sine of the angle between the ecliptic and the celestial equator</term> is produced.

The procedures of calculation for the sake of **precision** <note>original: "sūkṣmatva"</note> should be conceptualized in this manner. 34.

To demonstrate this conceptualization, he says: 
"When the Sun is positioned on the prime vertical <note>The 'sama-maṇḍala' or the great circle passing through the East, West, and Zenith</note>, the mathematician understands the **hour angle** <term>nata kāla: the time elapsed since the planet was at the meridian</term> in measures of **time** <term>nāḍī: a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term> through the application of logical reasoning <note>original: "yukti"</note>. O friend! Tell me the shadow of the **planet** <term>graha</term> and its **declination** <term>krānti</term> as understood by the wise."

Through the time elapsed since the meridian <note>original: "natakāla"</note> and the declination <term>krānti: the angular distance of the sun north or south of the celestial equator</term>, the mathematician <term>gaṇaka</term>—by the power of his reasoned logic—derives the observation. Starting from the midday of the **equinox** <term>viṣuva: the day when day and night are equal</term>, he brings the calculation forward as it is perceived. Thus, the mathematician consistently achieves the result from the heat of the sun's rays. Among those who know the **celestial sphere** <term>gola</term>, it is understood that this is how the shadow is established. 35.

To demonstrate the calculation of the whole, he states the formula for the shadow:

-> Take the square of the **radius** <term>trijyā</term> and the square of the **sine of the hour angle** <note>original: "natana-nāḍikā-jyā"</note>; find the difference between these two squares. Then, multiply by the **sine of declination** <term>krāntijyā</term> and divide by the radius. When this result is multiplied by the digits of the gnomon—which is **twelve** <note>In Indian astronomy, the standard vertical shadow-pole or gnomon is always 12 digits (aṅgulas) high</note>—and then divided by the hypotenuse, the length of the sun's shadow at that specific time is produced. <-

The explanation <note>original: "upapatti"</note> of this is as follows: The **sine of the hour angle** <term>natakāla-jyā</term> is located on the diurnal circle <note>the daily path of the sun</term>. It acts as the "base arm" <term>bhujajyā</term>. In this context, the radius is the hypotenuse. From the square root of the difference of their squares, we find the "perpendicular" or "cosine" of that position. 

<margin>Rama</margin> <note>This marginal note "Rama" is likely an invocation of the deity or the name of the scribe/commentator.</note>

From the diurnal circle, the sine of the hour angle is the base. In that place, by the measure of the radius, the calculation is performed. Through this proportion, the sine of the altitude is determined. By the logic of the **equinoctial shadow** <note>original: "viṣuvat-chāyā"</note> and the north-south movement, the precise position of the shadow is established. 4.

Through the sun's rotation, the measure of the shadow is determined. A wise scholar <note>original: "budhaḥ"</note> should bring the calculation to clarity by considering the projection. The **sine of the time** <note>original: "kāla-jīvā"</note> and the **radius** <term>tribhamorvyā: literally the "sine of three signs," which equals the 90-degree radius of the calculation circle</term> are used in conjunction with the difference in declination. The time elapsed since the meridian, once known, is multiplied by the difference in the **radius** <term>tribhamorvyā</term> and the **divisor of the latitude shadow** <term>akṣabhā-hṛti</term>.

...uct of the squares. The difference between the square of the **sine of the zenith distance** <term>nata-jyā: the sine of the angle of an object from the point directly overhead</term> and the square of the **radius** <term>trijyā</term> is calculated. That result is multiplied by the square of the **shadow** <term>bhā: the length of the shadow cast by the gnomon</term> and divided by the square of the product of the **radius** and the **twelve-digit gnomon**. 

This does not produce the square of the sine in its basic form. When the shadow and the sine are the dividend and the gnomon-divisor is used, the portion to be divided is reduced. By the difference of the squares of the **radius** and the **sine of the zenith distance**, multiplied by the square of the **equinoctial shadow** <term>palabhā: the shadow cast by a gnomon at noon on the equinox</term>, the result is joined. From this, the **result-sine** <term>phala-jyā: often referring to the sine of declination in this context</term> enters the calculation. Because of this reduction, the form of the dividend is simplified. Due to the destruction of that original form, the sage **Vasistha** <note>original: "vaśiṣṭhe"; likely referring to the Vasistha Siddhanta, an ancient astronomical treatise</note> explains the calculation of the **square of the result-sine** in four verses.

Through the knowledge of the square of the **result-sine**, he explains everything regarding the **sine of three signs** <term>tribhajyā: another term for the radius or sine of 90 degrees</term> in this context. Such is the nature of the derivation of the **square of the sine**. This is the meaning.

Regarding the **square of the sine of the arc** <term>rāśijyā-varga: the square of the sine value of a given zodiacal sign or arc</term>, [from the value] 9, one obtains the **square of the result-sine** <term>phala-jyā-varga: the square of the sine of declination or a derived longitudinal sine</term> in its natural form. However, the **square of the sine of declination** <term>krāntijyā-varga</term> remains as the **divisor** <term>hāra: the denominator in a fraction</term>, with a remainder of 45 at the start of the sign. In this place, the square of the result-sine acts as the divisor. To avoid errors in the calculation of the divisor, the square of the sine of declination is taken as this divisor, or alternatively, the square of the **radius** <term>trijyā: the sine of 90 degrees</term> is the base and the square of the result-sine is the **dividend** <term>bhājya: the numerator to be divided</term>. ||46||

From this divisor, the square of the sine of declination is indeed produced as the square of the result-sine; this is well-established in the geometric field. Therefore, from these ratios, this follows: here, the radius and the **sine of three signs** <term>tribhajyā: a term for the radius, as 3 signs equal 90 degrees</term> are considered. Here, in the form of the square of the sine of declination, there is a loss of the result-sine. Because there is no place for the original value, the square of the radius is substituted in the dividend in place of the square of the result-sine.

By using the square of the radius as the dividend, the divisor takes its previous form. Otherwise, it must be solved through the square of the radius. Because of this calculation involving the square of the radius, the divisor is multiplied by the square of the radius and divided by the square of the result-sine. Here, in showing the mentioned form of the divisor, in that specific form, the square of the radius is [marked by] 9... through such a form, I am bringing together their natural state as the divisor.

<page-num>906 / 106</page-num>

<warning>Slight fading and ink staining on the left margin. Modern pencil notation '106' added near original folio number '906'.</warning>

When it is established that the value is equal to the **square of the segment-sine** <term>khaṇḍajyā-varga: the square of the sine of a specific arc segment</term>, then the **square of the cosine** <term>koṭijyā-varga</term> is considered. In both segments, the **square of the sine of declination** <term>krāntijyā-varga; abbreviated as 'kajyā' in this text</term> is placed as the **divisor** <term>hāra</term>. Because of the equality between the multiplier and the divisor, there is a "destruction" <note>original: nāśa; refers to the mathematical cancellation of identical terms in the numerator and denominator</note> of both. In one instance, they remain as they are; elsewhere, in the second position, the square of the cosine is multiplied by the multiplier. 

In this second position of the divisor, the form of the **dividend** <term>bhājya</term> is established as having the nature of the square of the sine of declination. He explains the reason for this elsewhere, saying, "the divisor and the dividend..." Since the square of the sine of declination is present, the dividend is divided by the divisor, and the **square of the cosine-radius** <term>koṭi-trijyā-varga</term> is obtained as the result. Therefore, the dividend is considered to be the divisor multiplied by the result. 

In the second segments of the divisor, the square of the sine of declination is the measure, just as before. They say that the second segment of the divisor results in the square of the sine of declination. Here, in the place of the second segment, the square of the sine of declination is established by dividing [the term] by the sine of declination itself. 

<margin>Rāma</margin> <note>This marginal note likely refers to the commentator Rāma Daivajña or is a shorthand invocation.</note>

Thus, the segment of the divisor also takes the form of the square of the sine of declination. For this reason, it is said that the second segment of the divisor is produced from the ratio. The divisor here is established as being unbroken from the previous step. It should be in the form of the square of the sine... here, the result is the **sine of declination** <term>krāntijyā</term>.

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical and astronomical derivations concerning the relationships between the radius, sines, and various celestial arcs. ### On the Method of Calculation

He explains the method <term>nayana: the process of finding or calculating a value</term> by stating, "**This is it.**" The **radius** <term>trijyā: the sine of 90 degrees, often a fixed value in Indian astronomy like 3438'</term> divided by the **sight-line** <term>dṛksūtra: the hypotenuse of the shadow triangle or a line of sight</term> becomes the **cosine** <term>koṭijyā</term>. Thus, what was stated before is proven. (47) 

Now <margin>another</margin> he states another method: 

If the measure of the **equinoctial shadow** <term>palaprabhā: the shadow of a vertical gnomon at noon on the day of the equinox</term> is known, the mathematician should determine the **ascensional difference** <term>chara: the difference between the communal and sidereal rising of a sign</term>. By determining the measure of the **sine of declination** <term>krāntijyā</term>, the astronomers <note>original: jyotirvid</note> calculate these values. From the measure of the equinoctial shadow, the mathematician knows the **hypotenuse of the gnomon's shadow** <term>bhāskarṇa: literally "sun-ear," referring to the hypotenuse formed by the gnomon and its shadow</term>. Having known the **sine of the ascensional difference** <term>charajyā</term>, and having arrived at the measure of the sine of declination, the mathematicians who are experts in computation <note>original: kalpaka</note> state this clearly. (48)

At this point, he states another variation: The sine of the ascensional difference should be calculated here. The **radius** <term>trimovī: another term for trijyā</term> is divided, and from that square, after subtracting... the sine is added. The square root of that, multiplied by the **sine of the amplitude** <term>agrajyā</term> and multiplied by the **tangent** <term>sparśa: literally "touch," used here in a trigonometric context</term>, becomes the sine of declination. 

Thus, the sine multiplied by twelve and divided by the radius gives a result, the square of which is multiplied by the equinoctial shadow. Immediately following this, the **twelve-fold sine of the ascensional difference** divided by the sight-line results in the **declination**. (49)

To explain the reasoning <term>upapatti: mathematical proof or demonstration</term> clearly, he says:

The **earth-sine** <term>kujyā: the sine of the arc of the celestial equator between the six o'clock circle and the horizon</term> is produced from the sine of declination multiplied by the equinoctial shadow and divided by twelve. That [earth-sine] multiplied by the radius and divided by the **cosine of declination** <term>apama-siñjinī / dyujyā</term> results in the sine of the ascensional difference.

### The Logical Proof of Calculation

The logical proof <term>upapatti: a demonstration or derivation</term> for this calculation <term>ānayana: the process of reaching a mathematical result</term> should be understood as being aided by the previous derivation, or perhaps through **algebraic operations** <term>avyakta-kriyā: literally "working with the unmanifest" or unknown quantities</term>. 

As for the proof stated in the **Marichi** <note>The *Marīci* is a famous 17th-century commentary by Muniśvara on the *Siddhānta Śiromaṇi*, a foundational Sanskrit astronomical text</note>, it has already been established. For the sake of explaining the algebraic derivation, a question was posed here by my predecessors. <margin>107</margin> To explain that intent, he says:

As much as the **amplitude** <term>agra: the arc of the horizon between the east point and the sun at rising</term> is multiplied by the **sine of the diurnal circle** <note>original: dhajyā (likely dhṛti-jyā)</note> and divided by the **radius** <term>trijyakā: the sine of 90 degrees</term>, the result is the **earth-sine** <term>kujyā: a specific sine used in calculating the ascensional difference</term>.

When the **equinoctial shadow** <term>palabhā: the shadow cast by a 12-digit gnomon at noon on the equinox</term> is the base <term>bhuja</term> and twelve <note>the standard height of a gnomon</note> is the **perpendicular** <term>koṭi</term>, then if the earth-sine is the base, what is the perpendicular? By this proportion, that earth-sine is multiplied by twelve and divided by the equinoctial shadow; the result is the **sine of declination at the zodiacal entry** <term>saṅkrāntijyā</term>. (50)

To provide an alternative method, he explains the nature of the derivation. The product of the equinoctial shadow and the radius is divided by the total... <unclear>my own derivation</unclear>. By that divisor, twelve is multiplied and divided by the **radius**. Thus, the **sine of declination** <term>krāntijyā</term> is established through its previous form.

Now, he says this: "**Regarding one's own amplitude.**" <margin>Rāma</margin> <note>original: Rāmaḥ; likely a scribal invocation or the name of the copyist</note> To calculate one's own sine of declination, one relies on the sine of the shadow; thus, one relies on one's own amplitude. This is meant to show the possibility of the method. (51) This was well-reasoned in the previous conceptualization of the **long-standing tradition** <note>original: chira-hari</note>.

### The Iterative Calculation of the Shadow

Therefore, through the statement of the difference and the results, he says:

Triple the square of the first value, subtract it from twice the square of the result... <note>original: "प्रागस्ववर्ग त्रिगुणस्य... द्विमादिशोध्यापदेन"</note> and by the square root of that, the values are decreased or increased respectively. These are designated as the "prior" and the "subsequent" values. 

The first value is multiplied by **twelve** <note>arka: a synonym for the sun, also representing the number 12, the standard height of a gnomon</note> <insert>56</insert>. The result of that is then divided by the "subsequent" value. From that result, the **equinoctial shadow** <term>palabhā: the noon shadow of a 12-digit gnomon on the equinox</term> is determined. 

By adding and subtracting this again and again <note>punah punah: indicating an iterative mathematical process to reach a stable value</note>, one should calculate the sum. The direction is determined by the specific limb or part of the calculation. 

From twice the square of the **radius** <term>trijyā: the sine of a 90-degree arc</term> <gloss>the maximum sine value</gloss>, subtract the square of the intended result. The remainder is the base. The square root of this is used for the addition and subtraction, according to one's own intelligence, to determine the "prior" and "subsequent" values. 

In that process, the first value is multiplied by twelve and divided by the other. The result is the **equinoctial shadow**. The discrepancy in the sum is removed through this calculation. Because the initial assumption lacks "true" reality <note>vastvatvābhāvāt: because the initial seed value is only an approximation</note>, one must perform the calculation again to find the true **equinoctial shadow**. By following this stated method, one reaches a point where there is no further change in the result. In this way, the stable value of the **equinoctial shadow** arising from the twelve-digit gnomon is established.

### The Geometric Proof (Upapatti)

Now, the rationale for the beginning of the calculation is provided:

The proof for this is as follows: The given sum of the **perpendicular** <term>koṭi: the vertical side of a right-angled triangle</term> and the **base** <term>bhujā: the horizontal side</term> are placed... <note>original: "कोटिभुजापोर्त"</note>. These are accepted as being produced by the **radius** and the **twelve-digit gnomon**. The **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa: the diagonal of the right-angled triangle</term> is formed by the square root of the sum of those squares. Twice that square, reduced by the remainder...

### The Derivation of the Base and Perpendicular

Between these two, from the difference of the sines and the appearance of the angular difference, the **base and perpendicular** <term>bhujakoṭi: the horizontal and vertical components of a right-angled triangle in trigonometry</term> are produced through the step-by-step process <gloss>of those two</gloss>. Individually, these are processed through the sequence of sines and the circumference, by which the **perpendicular** <term>koṭi: the cosine or vertical component</term> is established. From this, the **twelve-digit gnomon-perpendicular** <term>dvādaśa-śaṅkukoṭi: a standard reference gnomon of 12 units used for shadow measurements</term> arises. By applying the proportion of the base to the fall of the shadow, the individual values are identified.

With the intended sum of the angles, the **equinoctial sine** <term>palajyā: the sine of the terrestrial latitude</term> <gloss>of the two</gloss> is found. From the sum of those individual values, and according to the direction of the **sun** <note>arkadiś: the direction of the sun, used here to orient the shadow</note>, the **equinoctial shadow** <term>palabhā: the shadow cast by a 12-digit gnomon at noon on the equinox</term> becomes equal.

If the result does not match the intended sum due to the direction of the sun, then through the calculation of that **equinoctial shadow**, the two quantities—the "prior" and "subsequent"—are established using the "eight-hand" method <note>this refers to a specific geometric or algebraic technique used in the Marīci commentary</note>. Within the limit of twelve, the state of the **equinoctial shadow** is determined to be neither more nor less. Through the proportion of the digits, twelve is used as the multiplier.

By the sum and difference of the **equinoctial shadows**, the two quantities are again established. For those instances where the shadow exceeds the standard measure, the calculation proceeds through the generation of those two quantities to find the sine. By the proportion of digits, the value of twelve is multiplied by **905** <gloss>905</gloss> and divided by the "prior" value <gloss>that</gloss>, resulting in an **equinoctial shadow** of **50** <note>these appear to be specific numerical examples used to demonstrate the formula</note>.

He concludes this section on the northern direction and the solar rule:

"When divided by the middle value, the result is greater than twelve—seventy indeed. Here, <gloss>repeatedly</gloss> after the initial calculation, the **equinoctial shadow** should be declared to the questioner <margin>Rāma</margin> by those skilled in calculation." 

Thus ends the section on the method. Regarding the northern region, the teachers say the following is the calculation.

### The Calculation of Declination and Sine

### 3. Calculations of the Sun in the Prime Vertical

When the **Sun** <note>original: "Sūrya"</note> has slightly passed or entered below the **Prime Vertical** <term>samamandala: the celestial circle passing through the east, west, and zenith points</term>, and when fifteen units of the day remain, an observer may not clearly perceive the exact position. If a man knows the **declination** <term>krānti: the angular distance of the Sun north or south of the celestial equator</term> of the Sun, he can determine the position. Some astronomers may proudly ask, "Who truly understands the movements of the heavenly bodies?" To answer this, the problem of the **zenith distance** <term>nata: the angular distance from the zenith</term> is addressed here with its solution. This has been established in the text of the ***Siddhanta Shiromani*** <note>Bhāskara II's foundational 12th-century work</note> for the sake of clarity. [62]

In response to this question, the author provides two half-verses to explain the method. One must determine the **sine of the hour angle** <term>natakālajyā: a trigonometric value representing the time elapsed since the celestial body crossed the meridian</term>. By squaring the desired value and considering the product of the radius and the gnomon's position, the square root provides the necessary calculation for the **hypotenuse of the shadow** <term>karṇa: in this context, the distance from the tip of the gnomon to the top of the shadow-casting object</term>. [63]

The calculation for the gnomon and other factors must be perfected. When the "Thousand-Rayed One" <note>original: "Sahasra-rashmi," a poetic name for the Sun</note> is positioned in the **Prime Vertical**, the calculation becomes clear. This has been explained by me with full logical proofs in my commentary titled ***The Ray of Light*** <note>original: "Marīci," Munīśvara’s famous 17th-century commentary on the Siddhānta-śiromaṇi</note>. The wise scholars should examine the derivation provided there. [64]

Here, what was intended for the "King of the Oceans" <note>likely a reference to a specific patron or a metaphor for a vast subject</note> is shown. He says: when the **Sun** <note>original: "Mārttaṇḍa"</note> enters the **Prime Vertical**, a small shadow of eight **digits** <term>aṅgula: a traditional unit of measurement, roughly the width of a finger</term> was observed. At what place and at what time did this occur? This involves calculating the difference in the **sine of declination** <term>krāntijyā</term>...

### On the Necessity of Spherical Astronomy

<note>original: "Sakalakam"; likely referring to the entirety of the system</note> Everything regarding the positions of the **spherical earth** <note>original: "Gola-kṣiti"</note> and its degrees is clearly established. Therefore, O **mathematician** <term>gaṇaka: one who calculates; an astronomer-astrologer</term>, who among those proficient in calculation is able to speak with authority without [understanding] the sphere? Pure mathematical thought alone is not enough to cross the obstacles of doubt; it is like a serpent. 

Within the famous **three branches** <term>skandhatraya: the three divisions of Jyotiṣa—Siddhānta (astronomy), Saṃhitā (omens), and Horā (astrology)</term>, the **Sphere** <term>Gola: spherical astronomy or the armillary sphere</term> is well-known. Without a description of the sphere, the determination of the truth of mathematics remains incomplete. The understanding of the mathematical principles is dependent upon the description of the sphere; without this, the **prediction of results** <term>phalādeśa: the astrological interpretation of astronomical data</term> does not arise accurately.

From the sphere, the **Ascendant** <term>lagna: the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon</term> and the others are derived. These are the famous **twelve houses** <term>bhāva: the astrological houses that determine different areas of life</term>. These houses are the primary cause for the declaration of results. Just as a lotus is born from mud, these results arise from the houses, which are joined by the twelve **junctions** <note>original: "sandhibhiḥ"; the cusps or boundaries between astrological houses</note> imagined constantly [upon the sphere]. 

The established position of the sphere is the proof. Without this determination, the pride of one's predictions is like a tree without roots. Here, the primary Ascendant and the position of **Mars** <note>original: "Kuja"</note> are considered; their forms are essential to the Ascendant.

All of this has been described according to its true nature in the **Siddhānta Śiromaṇi** <note>original: "Crest-jewel of Treatises"; the definitive 12th-century work by Bhāskarācārya</note> within the **Chapter on the Three Problems** <term>Tripraśnādhikāra: the section dealing with the three problems of time, place, and direction</term>, along with the **rationale** <term>vāsanā: the underlying physical or geometric explanation of a mathematical rule</term>. In accordance with that authority, the determination of other matters is also settled. If one considers the houses... <unclear>...two-fold or six-fold...</unclear> the mathematical groups...

It asserts that the twelve astrological houses and predictions (Phalādeśa) must be grounded in the geometric rationale found in Bhāskara’s Siddhānta Śiromaṇi.</summary>

### On the Nature of the Four Houses

Regarding the statement "of the nature of the fourth [house]," and so on: the **Ascendant** <term>Lagna: the 1st house or eastern horizon</term>, the **Fourth**, the **Seventh**, and the **Tenth** houses are established through calculation. By the word "only," the eighth house and others are excluded from this specific category. Their nature is established by the difference in the calculated parts; thus, it is understood that they are proven through mathematics and are indeed accurate. 

Now, one might ask: "Since these are used for the **prediction of results** <term>phalādeśa: the interpretive side of astrology</term>, and are established by our own reasoning, do they lack traditional authority? Or rather, are they not based on the words of the sages?" 

To this, the author replies: **"These matters are explained here."** Even in the **Compendium** <term>Saṃhitā: a branch of Jyotiṣa dealing with omens and collective destiny</term> and the **Mathematical Treatises** <term>Gaṇita-grantha</term>, the order of these calculations is described. In various regions, the divisions—consisting of parts, degrees, and so on—are stated. 

These **divisions** <term>vibhāga: sections of the celestial circle</term> exist within the sign. Here, one should imagine them as being measured by an interval. This **prediction of results** <term>phala</term> is woven together like a net of jewels <note>original: "ratna-yoga-rupa"; a metaphor for the interconnectedness of planetary combinations</note> situated within the cosmic structure. 

These mathematical procedures regarding the divisions and other factors are unique. In the western portion of the calculation... <margin>Rama</margin> <note>Likely the name of a commentator or scribe</note> ...because of the lordship of the signs, they state the measurement. Following the sequence of the degrees, there are divisions for the signs like Aries and Libra. There are twelve such **conceptual divisions** <term>dvādaśa-prakalpa: the twelvefold arrangement of the zodiac</term> belonging to the youth <note>original: "Kumāra"; possibly referring to the planet Mars or a specific mythological figure associated with the calculation</note>. These exist in the form of a combination of jewels, situated within the north and south positions of the celestial sphere.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the mathematical derivation of astrological houses, specifically contrasting different schools of thought and affirming the authority of the sage Parashara regarding the four cardinal angles (Kendras).</meta>

Whatever is to be written here applies to each individual division and the portion of the space. This is how the results of those positions are maintained. 

There should be no confusion regarding the standard measurement starting from **Aries** <term>Meshadi: the beginning of the zodiacal sign Aries, used as a reference point</term>. The measurement of the **astrological house** <term>Bhava: a "house" or "state," representing a specific area of life</term> is determined in this way for the purpose of identifying **auspicious and inauspicious results** <note>original: "shubhashubha phala"</note>. This is what is meant when we speak of the practical application of these measurements. (85)

The nature of the houses has been explained. Because the houses are established based on these intentions, those who imagine different house-circles often fall into great confusion and desire. Some claim there are seventeen houses, but these are said by others and are not established by the traditional astronomical proofs. One should not be distracted by the many different options proposed by various teachers. (86)

Regarding the calculation of the **Ascendant** <term>Lagna: the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon</term> and the nature of the houses, much has been said by various authors. However, by the grace of the Sun, the divisions established by the ancient authorities remain the standard. Regarding the houses mentioned by others, we look to the **Horoscopy of Parashara** <note>Parashara is the legendary sage and foundational authority of Indian astrology</note> and similar foundational texts. 

In these primary texts, the **first, fourth, seventh, and tenth houses** <note>these are the "Kendras" or angles, considered the pillars of a horoscope</note> are established through **spherical astronomy** <term>Gola: the study of the celestial sphere</term> and **mathematical methods** <term>Ganita: calculation or arithmetic</term>. Other houses, as proposed by different schools, may be mathematically derived but their specific results are known through the teachings of the wise. It is said that...

### The Calculation of the Tip of the Gnomon

Regarding the calculated position of the **tip of the gnomon** <note>original: "shankvagram," the point marking the shadow's extremity, used to determine celestial coordinates</note>, let the value be 88. From the **square of the radius** <note>original: "trijya varga," the standard radius used in Indian trigonometry, usually 3438 minutes</note>, one should subtract the square of the gnomon-tip's distance; the square root of the remainder is then used for the calculation of the **houses** <note>original: "bhava," the twelve divisions of the ecliptic</note>. 

Through the measurement of the signs and the **sines** <note>original: "jya"</note> of the zodiacal positions, the value 66 is used as a gateway to determine the **seventh house**. In the arc of the bow, those which are not yet reached must be established from the previous house. In this place, there are six distinct reasons or causes that determine the differentiated results of the houses.

### Methods of Establishing the Houses

As stated in the first section, the houses must be established correctly. This includes the **ninth house**, the **eleventh house**, and the **twelfth house**. Following the earlier method of the ancestors, and specifically the methods described in the **Chapter on the Three Questions** <term>Triprashna Adhikara: a standard section in Sanskrit astronomical treatises (Siddhantas) dealing with time, direction, and place using a gnomon</term>, one should first calculate the **gnomon** <note>original: "shanku," a vertical pillar used to cast a shadow for astronomical observation</note>. 

From that gnomon, one finds the "gnomon-foot" <note>original: "shanku-tala," the distance from the center of the horizon to the base of the gnomon’s projection</note>. The houses are then oriented by determining whether they are southern or northern in their declination. 

One should then divide the values accordingly. Then, taking the squares of the gnomon and its shadow-projection, and comparing them to the **square of the radius**, the root is found. Through this method, the values for the **seventh house** and the four subsequent houses are to be established. 

### Determining the Intermediate Houses

Then follows the **calculation** <note>original: "karma"</note>:
*   **Tenth House** <note>original: "dashama bhava"</note>
*   **Eighth House** <note>original: "ashtama bhava"</note>
*   **Ninth House** <note>original: "navama bhava"</note>
*   **Eleventh House** <note>original: "ekadasha bhava"</note>
*   **Twelfth House** <note>original: "dvadasha bhava"</note>

By looking at the interval between these two—the southern and northern points—the proximity of the houses is determined. For the **eighth house**, the sines are generated from the northern latitude. For the **ninth house**, the sines are generated from the inner proximity. In this same manner, the **twelfth** and **eleventh** houses are also derived...

### Conclusion of the Calculation for the Tenth House

Regarding the calculation of the two **tenth houses** <note>original: "dashama-bhava," the house associated with the zenith or midheaven</note>, through this cycle of 66 and in the sequence of 18, this particular matter is concluded. 

By the seventh and the fifth, the inquiry into the two zodiac signs is 9. From the remainder of the results of the **Phalguni** constellation <note>original: "phalgu-rupayai," referring to the lunar mansion used as a reference point</note>, the **tenth house** is diminished by the result of the division. The **eighth and ninth houses** <note>original: "ashtama-navama-bhava"</note> arise when there is a loss in the "merit" <note>original: "dharma-hanau"; in Indian astrology, the ninth house is often called the house of "Dharma" or merit</note>; the tenth house remains equal to them. 

The tenth house is the <unclear>middle</unclear> house, joined with the calculated results of the eighth and ninth. In the same manner, the eleventh and twelfth houses are joined with the results produced by the "merit." Thus, the tenth house is joined with the **eleventh and twelfth houses** <note>original: "ekadasha-dvadasha-bhava"</note>. In this way, the 13th part of the sequence is established.

### The Precise Nature of the Intermediate Houses

The eighth, ninth, eleventh, and twelfth houses are **precise** <note>original: "sukshma," meaning mathematically refined or minute</note> and are established through the **nature of the arrangement of the celestial sphere** <term>gola-samsthana-svarupa: the conceptual model of the armillary sphere or the globe used to prove astronomical coordinates</term>. 

Following this, these four houses—the eighth, ninth, eleventh, and twelfth—are joined with six signs <note>adding 180 degrees to find the opposite point on the zodiac</note> in their proper sequence. <margin>Precise</margin> These then become the second, third, fourth, fifth, and sixth houses, which are also precise.

### Mathematical Derivation of the Houses

If those degrees are known to be 6, then from that the **square** <term>varga: the product of a number multiplied by itself</term> is taken; from the square root of that quantity—**93**—the calculation proceeds. By its own nature, through the six seasons <note>referring to the six-part division of the year or circle</note>, the **ascendant** <note>original: "lagna": the point of the ecliptic rising on the eastern horizon</note> is established in its proper place.

From the revolving circles above the horizon, the **eleventh and twelfth houses** <note>original: "ekadasha-dvadasha-dani," the houses preceding the ascendant in the sky</note> are determined. In the positions of their own values, they should be calculated according to the intention of the **sines** <term>jyā: the chord-half or sine function essential to Indian trigonometry</term>. Because of the constant rotation of the celestial sphere, one should not consider these positions as fixed; this is the meaning.

### The Interval on the Ecliptic

In this context, concerning the previously taught regions of the **ecliptic** <term>krānti-vṛtta: the 'circle of declination' or the path of the sun</term>, the interval between the two points is described. Because of this, the position of one's own latitude is determined by the previous method. In the region where the **ascendant** is equal, that position is the **Zenith** <term>kha-madhya: literally 'the middle of the sky,' the point directly overhead</term>.

The degrees of the interval between these two points are known as the **denominator of the root** <note>original: "mūla-hara"</note>. As has been stated, through the operation of calculation, the perfection of this divisor is achieved. The **demonstration of the remainder** <note>original: "avasheṣopapatti": a mathematical proof explaining the values left over after division</note> is not concluded here, but the values are established as previously stated on the earth.

According to the explanation of the **tenth house** <note>original: "dashama": the midheaven or house of the meridian</note> and the latitude, and by the distribution of the light, the calculation of the zodiac signs and their **sines** <note>original: "guṇa": here referring to the trigonometric multipliers</note> is performed without deviation.

### The Principle of the Tenth House

Therefore, the value **25** is the fundamental principle. From the **ascendant** <note>original: "lagnādi," the rising sign</note> and subsequent points, the **tenth house** <term>daśamabhāva: the Midheaven, or the highest point the sun reaches in its daily path</term> is first established in the middle of the **true house position** <term>sphuṭabhāva: the mathematically corrected position of an astrological house</term>. Through this, the value again becomes **25**. When combined with the **measure of the house's extent** <note>original: "bhoga-māna"</note>, this calculation yields the true house position. 

According to the **consideration of the celestial sphere** <term>gola vicāra: the study of spherical astronomy and the movement of planetary bodies</term>, there is no variation in this result. This is established for the sake of determining the positive and negative mathematical values through the denominator. 

### Sines and Declinations

If the interval between the true inherent nature and the tenth house is obtained as the **sine of declination** <term>jyākrānti: the sine of the angle of a planet's distance north or south of the celestial equator</term>, then the true value is found. At that time, through the difference between the sines of the **three signs** <term>virāśi: here referring to 90 degrees or a quarter of the zodiac</term> and the specific signs used in the calculation, the result is determined. The **sine of the signs** <term>rāśija-jyā</term> should be placed in the tenth house to ensure the calculation remains consistent with the celestial rotation.

<margin>Rama</margin> <note>The scribe has written the name of the deity Rama in the margin, a common practice to seek divine blessing or as a signature.</note>

This result is obtained from the movement of time and the resulting mathematical falling <note>referring to the calculation of planetary descent or position</note>. The result is a positive value for the house position located in the middle region between two house boundaries. Therefore, through the calculation of the declination, the desired **extent of the house** <term>bhāva bhoga māna: the longitudinal span or 'enjoyment' of a specific astrological house</term> is made clear. 

As it has been stated, the desired value arises from the extent of the house's true nature. When combined with the signs in the true house measures, the extent of the true house is revealed. In these two values, the success of the calculation is achieved through the **consideration of the arrangement of the celestial sphere** <note>original: "gola saṃsthāna vicāra"</note>.

<meta>This page transitions from the technical calculation of the 10th house (the Midheaven) to the broader conceptual framework of the "house junctions" and the effective power of planets as they move through these divisions. ## The Determination of the House Junctions

Therefore, having defined the houses, a question is raised: If the inherent nature has been described in the **determination of the houses** <term>bhāva-sādhana: the mathematical process of establishing the twelve houses of a horoscope</term>, then why has the same not been said for the **position of the junctions** <term>sandhi-sthāna: the boundary point where one astrological house ends and the next begins</term>? 

To this, the author replies: In this calculation of the houses, there is no distinction made through such means. When a planet is equal to the center of the house, it is understood by the people to have its **full result** <term>sampūrṇa-phala: the maximum astrological influence or strength of a planet</term>. 

### The Span and Strength of the Houses

Regarding the **starting point of the result** <term>phalārambha-sthāna</term> and the location of the result, some adjustments must be made. In that context, because the **span of the house** <term>bhāva-bhoga: the total degrees or "area" over which a house exerts its influence</term> possesses the full result at its center, the starting point of one house is the point where the previous house's result ends and the next house's result begins. 

This is an established principle in the conceptualization of these divisions. In the specific section concerning the calculations, the measurement of the **house junction** <term>bhāva-sandhi</term> is joined to the house center. For this reason, in the opinion of the manual-makers, the span of the house is not measured solely by its middle position. Thus, the description of the junctions and the mathematical results are consistent. 

### The Authority of the Twelve Divisions

Now, an objection is raised regarding the authority of the **teachings of the sages** <note>original: "ārṣa," referring to the ancient Vedic seers or Rishis</note> concerning these seven houses. To clear this doubt, the text relies on the geographical location and the teachings of the sage Vashistha. 

The house results arise from the specific region. From the position of the **ascendant** <term>lagna: the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon</term>, the declination is considered. These divisions, which are based on the **Aries and the other signs** <term>Meṣādi: the sequence of the zodiac beginning with the sign of Aries</term>, are not merely arbitrary; they are the dwellings of the results. For the sake of determining these results, starting from the specific calculated position, the seven divisions proceed until they reach a total of **twelve in number** <term>dvādaśa-saṃkhyākāḥ</term>, encompassing the entire celestial sphere.

<summary>This page explains the "Bhava-Sandhi" (house junctions), clarifying that while a planet's power is strongest at the center of a house, its influence begins and ends at mathematical boundaries that link the twelve signs together starting from Aries.</summary>

<keywords>house junctions, bhāva-sandhi, full result, bhāva-bhoga, twelve signs, Aries, ascendant, Vashistha, astrological calculation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of astrological house divisions, shifting from the calculation of the house centers to the "states" (avasthās) of planets and the terrestrial calculations involving distances (yojanas) and the celestial sphere.</meta>

## The Starting Points of Planetary States and Results

The starting point for the **planetary states** <term>śayanādi-avasthā: a system of twelve states, such as 'resting' (śayana), that describe a planet's temporary condition and strength</term> begins from the region of **Aries** <note>original: "meṣādi pradeśam"</note>. Because of this, the counts proceed accordingly. These are established as the **point of completion for a location** <term>sthāna-samāpti: the boundary where one division of the zodiac ends</term>. 

They are not like the **middle of the location** <term>sthāna-madhya: the center point where a house's influence is strongest</term> described in previous sections, nor are they merely the points of commencement. After the signs starting from **Aries**, the results are written by the best of astrologers. 

### Authority of the Compendiums

In the established **astrological compendiums** <term>saṃhitā: the branch of Vedic science dealing with mundane astrology and natural omens</term>, these positions are recognized as the cause for **auspicious and inauspicious results** <note>original: "śubhā-śubha phala"</note>. 

<margin>Rāma</margin>
<note>This marginal note likely refers to Rāma Daivajña, a prominent 16th-century commentator on astronomical texts.</note>

Regarding the determination of the days and the food consumed... for the purification of the self. There, the calculation is known by the **wise man** <note>original: "sudhīman"</note>. From the middle of one's own country to the various directions, the distance is measured in **yojanas** <term>yojana: an ancient unit of distance, approximately 8 to 9 miles</term>. This refers specifically to the **distance between locations** <term>deśāntara: the longitudinal difference between two places, essential for correcting planetary positions from a prime meridian</term>. 

### The Wisdom of the Sphere

O wise one! It might be said that even for an intelligent person, this is difficult to speak of without the proper beginning of the calculation. However, for those skilled in the **science of the sphere** <term>gola-śāstra: the study of spherical astronomy and the celestial globe</term>, the contemplation of these matters becomes possible. 

The address "O Wise One" is intended for one who is capable of the **deliberation on the sphere** <note>original: "gola-vāsanā-vicāra"</note>, meaning the underlying astronomical rationale and spatial logic of the heavens.
